\textbf{Recursive Information--Container Dynamics: A Multiscale Theory
of Adaptive Systems}

\emph{RICD 17.4 --- Catalog of Mechanisms}

This catalog is the companion to the RICD 17.4 spine. It holds the
mechanisms, edge cases and audit protocols that extend the core engine,
each under its section number, so that a reference to a section or item
not found here refers to the spine. Each mechanism enters the engine
through the spine's extension points (the resource-cost sum M\_add(t),
§7.3, and the forcing sum F\_total(t), §6.4) or as a modifier with a
stated inactive value (§6.1, Definition 10.5b.18.14a; §8.6a's
𝒱\_\{i→j\}; §10.3a's anchor capacity A), or through the variables it
defines, and a domain adapter needs only the mechanisms that apply to
its domain. Where a definition now stated in the spine stood in one of
these sections, a note in its place says where it is.

\hypertarget{part-8.-adaptive-network-dynamics}{%
\section{Part 8. Adaptive Network
Dynamics}\label{part-8.-adaptive-network-dynamics}}

\hypertarget{preferential-attachment-toward-cohesive-clusters}{%
\subsection{8.3.1 Preferential Attachment Toward Cohesive
Clusters}\label{preferential-attachment-toward-cohesive-clusters}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.3.1 (First-Contact Attraction).}}

For an uncoupled or newly-decoupled container C\_new evaluating
potential first contact among containers or clusters \{C\_k\} within
observable range, define the attraction weight

A\_first(C\_new, C\_k, t) = ν\_pref · Λ\_int(C\_k, t), ν\_pref
\textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where Λ\_int(C\_k, t) is §9.10.1's internal cohesion measure, read here
as an externally observable signal of a candidate's own reciprocal
coupling density rather than only as a self-referential containment
mechanism. A solitary candidate, with no internal membership to measure
cohesion over, has Λ\_int(C\_k,t) = 0 by Definition 9.10.1 ---
attraction under this definition requires visible evidence of actual
reciprocal engagement with others, not merely a stable-looking
presentation, which a single container cannot manufacture unilaterally
regardless of how calm its own σ\_o reads.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.3.1 (Preferential Attachment).}}

New or newly-uncoupled containers entering a network form their first
live edge (crossing Ξ's threshold, §8.3) with candidate C\_k at a rate
increasing in A\_first(C\_new, C\_k, t). First contact is therefore
systematically biased toward already-cohesive clusters, not distributed
uniformly across candidates and not driven by whichever candidate
presents the calmest official signal. This is the formal content of new
entrants being drawn toward an already-gathered group rather than toward
an individually calm-presenting container: the attraction is to a
cluster's visible, reciprocally-produced coupling density, which is a
harder signal to fake alone than a stable σ\_o.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 8.3.1.1 (Predator Exclusion by Construction).}}

A high-entropy container acting alone, without co-opted or captive
members contributing to its own internal cohesion, presents Λ\_int(C,t)
= 0 under Definition 9.10.1's null-membership convention, and therefore
supplies zero first-contact attraction under Definition 8.3.1 regardless
of how low its σ\_o reads externally. This gives grid-level predator
starvation a formal mechanism rather than requiring it be stipulated: as
cohesive low- and medium-entropy clusters grow denser (rising Λ\_int),
they become preferentially more attractive to new entrants under
Proposition 8.3.1, while an isolated high-entropy container,
structurally incapable of generating a comparable signal on its own,
receives proportionally fewer first contacts over time. This is not
exclusion by a rule targeting predators specifically; the attraction
mechanism was simply never available to a container with nothing genuine
to reciprocate.

\hypertarget{a-coupling-valence-a-classification-of-inter-container-relationships}{%
\subsection{8.5a Coupling Valence: A Classification of Inter-Container
Relationships}\label{a-coupling-valence-a-classification-of-inter-container-relationships}}

A coupling between two containers can be classified by its net effect on
each party's own trajectory --- concretely, whether the coupling's
contribution to each party's dd\_A/dt (§10.3) is positive, negative, or
negligible. This gives every pairwise coupling a sign for each side, (+,
-, or 0), and the resulting sign pairs correspond to the seven classical
ecological interaction types, each of which already has, or is given
below, a specific formal mechanism in this document rather than
remaining a loose analogy.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Mutualism (+/+):} both parties' trajectories benefit. Mutual
  Anchor Clusters (§10.5b.13.1, Φ\_mutual, Γ\_mutual) are this
  document's mechanism --- reciprocal stabilization neither member could
  sustain alone.
\item
  \textbf{Commensalism (+/0):} one party benefits, the other's
  trajectory is unaffected. A plausible candidate mapping --- a
  low-resource container anchoring near a much larger, stable one whose
  own trajectory is unmoved by the coupling --- has not been separately
  verified against this document's anchor-eligibility machinery
  (§10.5b.13) and should be treated as unconfirmed rather than
  established.
\item
  \textbf{Parasitism (+/-), chronic:} ordinary extraction (§8.6a below)
  --- the predator gains, the host is weakened but continues
  functioning, resource debt accumulating over an ongoing coupling.
\item
  \textbf{Predation (+/-), acute:} the same sign, but the interaction
  crosses into the target's own collapse machinery (§10.5b.9d,
  disintegration, or a full tipping cascade) rather than remaining
  within ongoing extraction dynamics --- the distinguishing mark is
  whether the coupling stays inside §8.6a's extraction dynamics or
  triggers the target's own collapse resolution.
\end{itemize}

Scope, not only sign, determines whether predation is entropic. Sign
alone --- (+/-) --- does not determine whether a predation relationship
is itself a sign of entropic misalignment. Predation directed at a
container outside i's own immediate coupled network, sustained as part
of a broader grid-level cycle in which the target population retains
genuine adaptive possibility and the capacity to develop its own
countermeasures over time (§10.5b.2.8, below), can be a stable,
negentropic element of the larger grid --- a food web that never removes
prey's own capacity to adapt is not, at the grid level, evidence of
divergence. Predation or destruction directed at a container within i's
own immediate coupled network, by contrast, is a positive sign of
entropic misalignment unless demonstrably defensive, independent of
whether the broader grid remains stable. Scope and sustained mutual
adaptability, not sign alone, are what separate a healthy predator-prey
cycle from entropic destruction wearing the same mathematical sign. Two
further, real criteria sharpen this beyond scope alone, and both cut
across the ecological categories above rather than tracking them:
taxonomy (predator, parasite, symbiont) does not by itself determine
entropic status, and a biologist or ecologist reading only the category
labels above would be right to object that predation and parasitism are
not inherently entropic phenomena. They are not. Two real, independently
checkable factors determine it instead. First, metabolic efficiency
(η\_i, §8.6a): can the extracting system actually use the energy it
takes, or does the extraction destroy or weaken a target without the
extractor being able to convert what it took into anything usable? A
high-η\_i transfer -\/- the extracted resource fully, lastingly absorbed
into the receiving system's own continued function -\/- is structurally
negentropic regardless of how predatory the interaction looks on the
surface (a redback male's own body converted directly into offspring
viability, with no unmetabolized remainder). A low-η\_i transfer, where
most of what is taken cannot actually be used and converts instead to
destructive heat requiring venting (§10.5b.19.7's Self-Corrosion), is
structurally entropic regardless of whether the interaction is between
different species or the same one -\/- the predator or parasite is
damaged by its own extraction nearly as much as, or in some cases more
than, the cost it imposes on the target. Second, homeostatic function:
does the interaction operate as a genuine negative feedback loop at the
wider grid level, or does it push the grid toward imbalance? A real,
checkable pair of conditions distinguishes the two. Target selection
bias: does the extracting system preferentially couple with targets
whose own D\_op(j,t) or δR(j,t) was already elevated before the coupling
began -\/- removing already-struggling or already-dying members, the
Armillaria case, a fungal network that specifically kills and consumes
weakened or diseased trees, clearing space for healthy new growth as
part of forest renewal -\/- rather than targeting indiscriminately or
preferentially targeting healthy, thriving members. Post-removal grid
capacity: does the network's own resilience functional (R\_N, §8.9) or
aggregate adaptive capacity for the remaining members trend upward
following successful predation events, consistent with a genuine renewal
function, or does it trend downward, consistent with net depletion
rather than regulation. Where both point toward renewal -\/- targeting
pre-existing dysfunction and improving remaining grid capacity -\/- the
interaction is genuinely homeostatic and negentropic at the grid level
even though it destroys the individual target; where either fails, the
interaction is destabilizing regardless of its ecological category.
Efficiency and homeostasis are independent axes, not two names for the
same test: a coupling can be efficient at the individual level while
still destabilizing the wider grid (efficiently draining healthy,
thriving members rather than struggling ones), or genuinely homeostatic
at the grid level while wasteful at the individual one (an inefficient
removal of a diseased member that still frees real capacity for the
network overall).

A third, distinct axis belongs alongside efficiency and homeostatic
function, since it explains a real category of harm neither of the first
two axes captures: extraction or harm produced by institutional
incoherence rather than by any single knocking container. Where multiple
containers \{k\_1, ..., k\_n\} each independently couple to and act upon
a shared target j (a shared patient, a shared dependent, a shared
institution under joint oversight), and the coupling strength between
those acting containers themselves is low -\/- Λ\_\{k\_a,k\_b\}(t) ≈ 0
for the acting containers pairwise, even while each Λ\_\{k\_a,j\}(t)
individually is substantial -\/- a predictable, quantifiable entropic
outcome for j can arise even where every individual k\_a is, on its own,
low-entropy and non-predatory. Definition 10.5b.11d (Siloed Aggregate
Forcing). Define the targets aggregate received forcing as Σ\_a
X\_\{k\_a,j\}(t), and the acting containers' own mutual coupling as
Λ\_silo(t) = mean\_\{a≠b\} Λ\_\{k\_a,k\_b\}(t). As Λ\_silo(t) → 0 with
Σ\_a X\_\{k\_a,j\}(t) held fixed or increasing, j's own hull-breach
inequality (§10.5a) is approached purely as a function of aggregate,
uncoordinated forcing -\/- no single k\_a need individually approach or
exceed any extraction threshold for j to be driven toward breach, since
no k\_a has access to the other's own contribution and therefore no
k\_a's own local view registers the compounding effect at all. This is a
predictable, structural outcome of low inter-actor coupling in a network
with a shared target, not a coincidental clustering of independently bad
actors: siloing between k\_1...k\_n is what makes the aggregate outcome
entropic, in the same direct sense that a single high-entropy
container's own internal incoherence produces its entropic outcome, just
relocated from within one container to across a poorly-coupled set of
them. The corrective implication follows directly from the mechanism
rather than needing separate derivation: raising Λ\_silo(t) among the
acting containers (real coordination, shared visibility into aggregate
forcing) is the structurally correct intervention, distinct from and not
achieved by improving any single k\_a's own individual entropy
classification, since the harm was never located in any one of them.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Competition (-/-):} both parties' trajectories are harmed by
  drawing on the same limited external resource, without a direct Λ\_ij
  coupling between them at all. §8.5a.1 below formalizes this; nothing
  in this document previously covered it.
\item
  \textbf{Amensalism (-/0):} one party harmed, the other unaffected and
  gaining nothing. A plausible candidate mapping --- a bystander
  container absorbing incidental momentum from a neighbor's cascade
  discharge (§8.8a, Drive\_B) without the discharging container
  benefiting from that harm --- has not been separately verified and
  should be treated as unconfirmed rather than established.
\item
  \textbf{Neutralism (0/0):} no meaningful coupling. Requires no
  separate mechanism; this is simply the case where Λ\_ij(t) is
  negligible or zero.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{a.1-competition-for-a-shared-external-resource}{%
\subsection{8.5a.1 Competition for a Shared External
Resource}\label{a.1-competition-for-a-shared-external-resource}}

Extraction (§8.6a) requires a direct coupling Λ\_ij(t) between predator
and target. Competition does not: two containers can harm each other's
trajectories by drawing on the same limited external resource pool
without any direct coupling between them at all --- a genuinely distinct
mechanism, not extraction under another name.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5a.1 (Shared Resource Pool).}}

Define a shared pool Ω\_pool(t) ≥ 0, external to any single container,
with its own regeneration dynamics:

dΩ\_pool/dt = γ\_pool·(Ω\_pool\^{}max − Ω\_pool(t)) − Σ\_\{k ∈
competitors\} D\_k(t), subject to the explicit nonnegativity constraint
Ω\_pool(t) ≥ 0 stated here as a real computational requirement, not left
to prose: a numerical implementation (an Euler step in particular) can
otherwise drive the continuous-time derivative below zero even with each
D\_k(t) individually capped by the current pool. Draws are suppressed as
the boundary is approached rather than permitted to overshoot it -\/-
formally, D\_k(t) is capped so that Σ\_\{k ∈ competitors\} D\_k(t) ≤
Ω\_pool(t) + γ\_pool·(Ω\_pool\^{}max − Ω\_pool(t))·Δt over any discrete
step of size Δt, a positivity-preserving flow rather than an uncapped
rate applied and corrected after the fact,

γ\_pool \textgreater{} 0 fitted, where \{competitors\} is the set of
containers drawing on this specific pool (adapter-defined membership ---
e.g., all containers within a given resource catchment or market), and
D\_k(t) is competitor k's actual draw.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5a.2 (Competitive Draw).}}

Each competitor's draw is capped by its own demand and by its share of
the pool relative to every other competitor's current effort:

D\_i(t) = min{[} demand\_i(t), share\_i(t)·Ω\_pool(t) {]}, share\_i(t) =
effort\_i(t) / E\_tot(t), E\_tot(t) = Σ\_\{k ∈ competitors\}
effort\_k(t), with the convention D\_i(t) = 0 for all i when E\_tot(t) =
0 -\/- a real, reachable case given the default effort is R\_i(t)
itself, so a population at zero resource produces exactly this state
-\/- since nobody is exerting resource-seeking effort and there is no
basis to allocate a share of the pool,

where effort\_i(t) ≥ 0 is an adapter-supplied measure of i's own
resource-seeking activity -\/- nonnegativity required explicitly, not
left to the semantic implication of the word "effort" alone, since
share\_i(t) is computed by ordinary division and a sufficiently
pathological adapter supplying a negative effort\_i(t) would produce a
negative share despite the quantity being named and used as one -\/-
defaulting to R\_i(t) (larger, more active containers claim
proportionally more of a contested pool by default, and R\_i(t) ≥ 0 by
§5.6's own convention, so the default itself always satisfies this).
D\_i(t) enters i's own E\_exch(t) (§7.4) as an ordinary resource inflow,
capped as above.

\emph{\textbf{Why this is genuinely (-/-).}} As the number of
competitors drawing on Ω\_pool(t) grows, or as aggregate effort rises,
each individual share\_i(t) falls for a fixed pool size, and D\_i(t)
falls correspondingly for every competitor simultaneously --- both, or
all, parties' trajectories are harmed by the same underlying pressure,
with no direct extraction relationship between any pair of them. This is
mechanically distinct from §8.6a's extraction: no container here is
drawing resource \emph{from} another container; every competitor is
drawing from the same external pool, and it is the pool's own
finiteness, not any other container's coupling, that produces the harm.

\hypertarget{b-network-topology-governance-and-forced-value-drift}{%
\subsection{8.5b Network Topology, Governance, and Forced
Value-Drift}\label{b-network-topology-governance-and-forced-value-drift}}

\emph{\textbf{Motivation.}} §8.5a classifies pairwise coupling
relationships. Nothing in the preceding sections characterizes the shape
a whole network of coupled containers organizes itself into --- whether
hierarchically concentrated or horizontally distributed --- independent
of any single pairwise relationship within it, or what that shape does
to containers forced to remain inside it.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5b.1 (Network Topology).}} Define two
distinct measures for a network of containers, deliberately kept
separate rather than combined into one quantity:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Structural centralization}, χ\_struct(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}: a graph
  measure of how concentrated the network's coupling connections are
  around a small number of nodes, independent of what flows along those
  connections.
\item
  \textbf{Resource-flow disproportion}, χ\_flow(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}: how far
  actual resource benefit received by central nodes departs from what
  their own genuine contribution would justify.
\end{itemize}

Structure alone cannot distinguish a legitimate coordinating hub from a
predatory one: a hub that does not personally benefit from its position
and one that extracts disproportionate benefit from the identical
position produce the same χ\_struct(t). The diagnostic is the
\emph{joint configuration} of the two measures, not either alone, and
not their numerical difference --- different (χ\_struct, χ\_flow) pairs
can share the same gap while describing radically different network
structures, so the gap itself does not uniquely encode the
classification below.

Proposition 8.5b.1 (Governance Configuration Correspondence). Renamed
from ``Governance as Regime Classification'' and the claim itself
softened: no explicit thresholds or regions mapping the (χ\_struct,
χ\_flow) plane onto the three entropy regimes are established anywhere
in this document, so the joint configuration does not mathematically
recover the existing three-way classification, which remains explicitly
grounded in σ\_o and σ\_p (§10.5b.1). What the joint configuration of
χ\_struct(t) and χ\_flow(t) genuinely provides is a
network-governance-level classification aligned with the entropy
regimes, useful as its own diagnostic axis, not a derivation that
recovers them from network topology alone:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Egalitarian (low-entropy baseline):} low χ\_struct(t); every
  member treated as structurally important; malfunctioning members
  receive help restoring function rather than removal, nonfunctioning
  members receive care rather than discard --- because discarding
  itself, not malfunction, is what this configuration treats as the
  divergence- causing act.
\item
  \textbf{Meritocratic (medium-entropy):} χ\_struct(t) may be
  substantial, but χ\_flow(t) stays proportional to genuine contribution
  --- a genuinely functional negentropic core (real reward for real
  contribution) coexisting with one specific tolerated harm (discarding
  members who are not malfunctioning, merely not contributing), the
  medium-entropy signature of one corrupted element within an otherwise
  intact system.
\item
  \textbf{Dominance hierarchy (high-entropy):} χ\_struct(t) high
  \emph{and} χ\_flow(t) high --- structured for the benefit of dominant
  nodes at direct, ongoing cost to subordinate ones, the gap between
  structure and earned benefit at its most extreme.
\end{itemize}

\emph{\textbf{Structural durability.}} Independent of governance
valence, sustained vertical (hierarchically concentrated) organization
is generally less durable than sustained horizontal (distributed)
organization, in conceptual systems as in physical ones --- a geological
mesa outlasting a spire is the same underlying principle expressed
structurally rather than informationally: concentrated structures carry
more of their own mass on a narrower base, and are correspondingly more
exposed to any single point of failure. Formalized directly in terms
already tracked elsewhere in this document, rather than left as analogy
alone: a container's exposure to single-point failure is approximated by
how concentrated its own structural support is, per χ\_supp (§10.5b.0's
geometric support ratio), against how much load that support currently
bears, per V(t):

Exposure(t) = V(t) / χ\_supp(t), Exposure(t) → ∞ as χ\_supp(t) → 0 for
fixed V(t) \textgreater{} 0 (a maximally concentrated, minimally
supported structure under load), Exposure(t) → 0 as V(t) → 0 for any
χ\_supp(t) \textgreater{} 0 (a fully horizontal structure carries no
concentrated load to expose in the first place). This recovers the
durability claim directly: a vertical structure (high V(t)) is less
durable specifically because it couples a high load to whatever support
base it has, while a horizontal structure (low V(t)) is durable
regardless of its support base's own size, since there is little
concentrated load for that base to need to carry.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5b.2 (Forced Value-Drift).}} A genuinely
negentropic container forced into sustained coupling within a
high-χ\_struct(t), high-χ\_flow(t) network --- unable to survive outside
it, per its own resource dependency on the hierarchy --- may begin
performing the dominant node's own values (form over function: optics
and compliance rather than genuine contribution) as a survival strategy,
without any formative-window shock or genesis-branching event (§10.5b.2)
having occurred. Sustained performance can become internalized belief:
the container's own feedback corrupts from the inside, purely as a
consequence of prolonged forced coupling, not developmental corruption.
Where this occurs independently across many nodes within the same
dominance hierarchy simultaneously, the result is a grid-level collapse
mechanism distinct from cascading extraction or synchronized
formation-stage corruption --- convergent, hierarchy-imposed value
corruption spreading through a network's own healthy members, driven by
the network's shape rather than by any single predatory act.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5b.3 (Functional Judgment Accuracy).}} A
system's own classification of a member as nonfunctioning is itself a
claim, subject to the same grounding test as any other self-assessment
(G(t), §6.3.2): internal agreement that a member is dispensable is not
evidence that the judgment is accurate, only that it is unanimous. Two
further considerations bear directly on whether such a judgment should
be trusted. First, a member's continued presence within the system is
itself weak evidence against the nonfunctioning label --- a system that
reliably discarded every genuinely useless member would not retain
members fitting that description, so persistence is data, not merely an
absence of correction. Second, apparent individual dispensability can
mask population-level function: a high-variance strategy for generating
outcomes --- biological or otherwise --- can produce mostly poor
individual results specifically because rare exceptional results are
what the strategy exists to generate, so culling on visible individual
performance can remove the population-level mechanism producing the very
outcomes worth keeping, without this ever being visible from any single
generation's data. Evolutionary variance in a genetic pool is the
clearest instance: individuals culled for apparently poor genetic
outcomes may be carrying the same high-variance strategy responsible for
the population's rare, exceptional successes: their removal would remove
the mechanism generating both without ever registering, from any single
generation's data, why it mattered.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5b.4 (Emergency Triage Validity).}}
Discarding nonfunctioning or low-functioning members is not
automatically an entropy signal where two conditions hold together, not
either alone. \textbf{Genuine existential constraint:} R(t) or δR(t)
(§7.3, §7.9) approaching a real floor, checkable against the system's
own resource state, not merely below some preferred target.
\textbf{Accurate triage judgment:} G(t) (§6.3.2) high for the specific
judgment being made --- the assessment of who is being deprioritized is
itself grounded, not distorted. Both present together is a legitimate
emergency mechanism, however costly; either absent collapses the action
into ordinary entropic neglect (Definition 8.5b.3). A system's own
sincere belief that it is engaged in emergency triage does not satisfy
either condition on its own --- sincere misapplication, invoking genuine
constraint or accurate judgment without either actually being present,
is itself a distortable claim, checkable the same way any other
self-assessment is.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.5b.5 (Standing Proxy Divergence).}} Where a
system optimizes toward a single proxy for value --- aggregate
contribution, measured utility, majority agreement --- as a
\emph{sustained, standing} policy rather than a bounded, one-time
application, the proxy and the reality it was meant to track can diverge
over time even absent any deception: the system becomes progressively
better at satisfying its own proxy while the gap between proxy and
underlying reality accumulates unchecked, exactly the condition G(t)
exists to detect and internal agreement cannot. This is the mechanism
behind why standing utilitarian-style governance (the meritocratic
classification, Proposition 8.5b.1) tends toward D\_op(t)-type
divergence while remaining, at every individual timestep, locally
optimal by its own measure --- the failure is cumulative and structural,
not a series of individually detectable errors. The same mechanism
applies directly to group agreement substituted for truth or accuracy at
grid scale: majority or consensus agreement was never evidence of
accuracy any more than a single container's internal agreement is
(G(t)'s core claim, restated at network scale), and a network whose
consensus has been captured by a predatory sub-population's manufactured
agreement (§10.5b.2's population-3 injection mechanism, operating here
at network rather than single-container scale) can converge an entire
grid on a verdict that is confidently, unanimously, and completely
wrong.

\emph{\textbf{Form substituted for function, a concrete instance.}} The
proxy need not be a measured quantity at all; institutional prestige
markers --- credentials, tenure, an established reputation --- can
themselves function as the standing proxy, substituted for the genuine
functional quality (a program's actual, currently-delivered value) they
were once a reasonable indicator of. Consider a hypothetical liberal
arts program whose faculty roster stays essentially unchanged for years
--- no departures forcing turnover, but also no new hires bringing
renewal --- while the program's own self-assessment continues to rest on
that same roster's accumulated prestige. Nothing here need involve
extraction, predation, or deliberate deception: the population sincerely
believes its long-established reputation for excellence is still an
accurate indicator of present quality, and that sincerity is precisely
the mechanism, not a mitigating factor. Believing form still tracks
function is what forecloses noticing the two have quietly come apart ---
the belief is not separate from the stagnation it produces, it is the
reason the stagnation stays invisible from inside the container
producing it. This is Standing Proxy Divergence acting through a
specific, common, and easily-overlooked proxy: prestige and tenure
standing in for currently-delivered quality, gap accumulating unchecked
for exactly as long as form continues to be mistaken for function.

\hypertarget{a.4-target-vulnerability}{%
\subsection{8.6a.4 Target
Vulnerability}\label{a.4-target-vulnerability}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.4 (Target Vulnerability).}}

Define 𝒱(j,t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}, target j's own true vulnerability to
extraction, as a decreasing function of j's own resilience signals:

𝒱(j,t) = 1 − {[} w\_Ψ·Ψ\_total(j,t) + w\_d·(d\_A(j,t)/d\_A\^{}max(j,·))
{]}, clipped to {[}0,1{]}, w\_Ψ + w\_d = 1, both fitted,

combining j's own negentropic population strength (Ψ\_total(j,t),
§10.5b.18.14) and j's own remaining adaptive possibility (d\_A(j,t),
§10.3). This is 𝒱\_true(j,t), j's actual latent state; a predator i does
not automatically have unmediated access to it, consistent with this
document's own separation of latent state from observation elsewhere.
The quantity a specific predator i actually acts on is 𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) =
O\_\{i→j\}(𝒱\_true(j,t)), i's own observation-mapped estimate, using
whatever access i genuinely has (A\_acc(i,j,t), §10.5b.18.9.1, reused
here rather than duplicated) --- a well-resourced predator with genuine
access approaches 𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) ≈ 𝒱\_true(j,t); an opportunistic
stranger with only surface-level cues (§8.6a.4's broadcast property)
works from a cruder estimate. Extraction dynamics elsewhere in this
document that reference 𝒱(j,t) should be read as depending on the acting
predator's own 𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t), not on unmediated access to 𝒱\_true(j,t),
except where a specific mechanism explicitly requires the latter. A
well-resourced but strongly negentropic-aligned, high-d\_A target and an
equally-resourced but weakly-aligned, low-d\_A target are no longer
treated identically by the extraction flux (§8.6a's formula): resource
size alone no longer determines how attractive a target is. 𝒱(j,t) = 1
recovers the original, resilience-blind formula exactly.

\emph{\textbf{Vulnerability as a broadcast property, not only a
persistent-coupling one.}} 𝒱(j,t) already depends only on j's own
current state (Ψ\_total(j,t), d\_A(j,t)) --- nothing in its definition
requires an existing coupling Λ\_ij, or any prior relationship between i
and j at all. This means low Ψ\_total(j,t) does not only impair j's own
perception of and response to a specific, already-coupled threat
(§10.5b.2.3, §10.5b.2.4); it makes j read as more exploitable to
\emph{any} predator's own opportunistic assessment, including one-off
encounters with no persistent coupling whatsoever --- a single,
unrepeated interaction in which an entirely unrelated predator applies
its own version of 𝒱(j,t) to a target it has never coupled with before
and will not couple with again. A container's own depleted negentropic
strength is not only an internal vulnerability to threats already inside
its network; it is externally legible, in the same currency, to threats
entirely outside it.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.4a (Disruption-History Flag).}} A
disruption history changes how other containers read a target. The
vulnerability a predator i acts on includes a term for j's visible
disruption history,

𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) = clip\_{[}0,1{]}( O\_\{i→j\}(𝒱\_true(j,t)) +
κ\_flag·Vis\_\{i→j\}·Z\_dis(j,t) ),

with Z\_dis(j,t) from Definition 10.5b.9a.5, Vis\_\{i→j\} ∈ {[}0,1{]}
the fraction of that history observable to i, and κ\_flag ≥ 0 fitted.
The flag decays only as Z\_dis(j,t) does, so it outlasts the episodes
that produced it. The term follows the flag account of repeat
victimization in criminology (Farrell and Pease), in which a target
persistently signals that it is attractive; it locates the source of
repeated targeting in the selecting container, not in the target's own
drive.

\emph{Domain instances.} Drought-stressed trees emit volatile cues that
bark beetles home in on, so the first stress draws the next attack;
predators preferentially take injured or diseased prey; a sovereign or
firm with a default history attracts vulture funds and predatory
lenders; a financially distressed college attracts acquirers and
revenue-share contractors.

\hypertarget{a.5-self-regulation-during-medium-entropy-ward-drift}{%
\subsection{8.6a.5 Self-Regulation During Medium-Entropy-Ward
Drift}\label{a.5-self-regulation-during-medium-entropy-ward-drift}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.5 (Predator Self-Regulation).}}

A high-entropy container's own rising Ψ\_total(i,t) (§10.5b.18.14) ---
its own negentropic-aligned population strengthening --- suppresses its
own extraction output directly, independent of any particular target's
vulnerability: the closing factor (1 − κ\_self·Ψ\_total(i,t))₊ in
§8.6a's extraction flux formula. This is a distinct suppression channel
from 𝒱(j,t) (§8.6a.4): a container drifting toward its own healing can
deliberately restrain itself even against a maximally vulnerable target,
which 𝒱(j,t) alone --- a property of the target, not the predator ---
cannot express. κ\_self = 0 recovers the unsuppressed formula.

\hypertarget{a.6-dormancy}{%
\subsection{8.6a.6 Dormancy}\label{a.6-dormancy}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.6 (Dormancy).}}

A high-entropy container i is dormant at time t when it has no active
extraction target: Σ\_j X\_\{i,j\}(t) = 0. By §10.5b.18.4's
construction, M\_decep,ext(t) = 0 throughout dormancy, since that term
is defined to be zero whenever i has no current extraction target.
Structural extraction dependence (§10.5b.18.25) during dormancy
accordingly reduces to the weaker condition

P\_prod(t) \textless{} M\_maint(C,t) + M\_decep,int(t),

omitting the external term entirely. A container for which
M\_decep,ext(t) was the deciding weight in an otherwise-structural
deficit becomes survivable in dormancy precisely because that weight
drops away --- the biological pattern of a parasite surviving host loss
by entering a low-activity holding state. A container for which
M\_decep,int(t) alone already exceeds available production, even at W(t)
= 0, remains structurally dependent regardless of dormancy: no reduction
in external activity relieves a purely internal deficit, and such a
container faces §10.5b.18.25's grid-starvation result whether or not it
has a target.

Dormancy is not an unconditional escape. §8.6a's own ``link-severing''
result already establishes that an isolated high-entropy container's
unvented metabolic heat H\_i(t) has nowhere left to go but back into its
own intrinsic driving force F\_i, directly feeding §10.5b's high-entropy
tipping mechanics. Dormancy relieves the resource dimension of
isolation; it does not relieve the heat-accumulation dimension. A
dormant container that survives its resource deficit may nonetheless be
accumulating pressure toward its own eventual tipping event throughout
the same dormant interval --- dormancy trades one risk for continued
exposure to the other, not a clean exit from both.

\emph{Domain instances.} Dormant parasites and latent viruses with no
active host extraction; a holding company with no current acquisition
target, maintaining its structure while it waits.

\hypertarget{a.7-positional-embedding}{%
\subsection{8.6a.7 Positional
Embedding}\label{a.7-positional-embedding}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.7 (Positional Embedding).}}

Active search (Σ\_search(i,t), §10.5b.11c.1) is a behavioral intensity
--- how hard i is looking. Positional embedding is a separate,
positional quantity: where i has placed itself in the network,
independent of any active search effort, and compatible with dormancy in
a way active search is not, since it costs nothing ongoing once the
positioning itself is established. Define E\_embed(i,t) ∈ {[}0,1{]},
adapter-supplied or defaulting to a function of i's current local
network density of low- and medium-entropy neighbors (richer
surroundings default to higher E\_embed). Passive, incidental target
encounter proceeds at

Σ\_passive(i,t) = κ\_passive · E\_embed(i,t) · κ\_density(i,t),
κ\_passive \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where κ\_density(i,t) is the resource-weighted density of viable targets
within i's network neighborhood. Crucially, Σ\_passive(i,t) can be
strictly positive while Σ\_search(i,t) = 0 --- a dormant container,
doing no active searching at all, can still ``stumble upon'' a target
purely through where it happens to sit, exactly the biological pattern
of a parasite embedding itself in a target-rich environment rather than
actively hunting.

\hypertarget{a.8-courting}{%
\subsection{8.6a.8 Courting}\label{a.8-courting}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.8 (Courting).}}

Define target attractiveness 𝒜(j,t) = 𝒱(j,t)·R\_j(t) (§8.6a.4) --- how
good a mark j currently is, combining vulnerability and resource size.
Courting is preferential allocation of i's existing search and embedding
effort toward high-𝒜 candidates, rather than uniform allocation across
all reachable candidates: among candidates \{C\_k\} within i's reach,
i's targeting probability is

P\_target(i,j,t) ∝ 𝒜(j,t), normalized over \{C\_k\},

so that Σ\_search(i,t) and Σ\_passive(i,t) (§8.6a.7) --- whose
\emph{overall intensity} is already set by i's own deficit and
positioning --- determine \emph{how hard} i looks, while courting
determines \emph{where} that effort is preferentially directed. This is
a targeting-weight modification, not a new intensity term.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.8a (Target-Specific Boost).}} An
extractor's reward memory is specific to the target that paid. Define
B\_ij(t) ≥ 0, i's accumulated reward from extraction from j,

dB\_ij/dt = κ\_B·η\_i·X\_\{i,j\}(t) − λ\_B·B\_ij(t), κ\_B, λ\_B
\textgreater{} 0 fitted,

and let it enter courting multiplicatively, P\_target(i,j,t) ∝ 𝒜(j,t)·(1
+ κ\_boost·B\_ij(t)), normalized over \{C\_k\}. A successful extraction
from j therefore raises i's targeting probability for j in particular,
not only i's general appetite (Z\_hun, §10.5b.19.1), following the boost
account of repeat victimization, in which the first event raises the
risk of the next because the extractor has learned that this target
pays. Where j denies reward (X\_\{i,j\} → 0), B\_ij decays at λ\_B and
the targeting rate falls; the flag (Definition 8.6a.4a) decays more
slowly, so a container can remain targeted after it has ceased to be
susceptible.

\emph{Domain instances.} Organizations that have been breached are
disproportionately breached again, partly because attackers already know
the route in; predators return to patches where foraging paid; lenders
return to borrowers who have paid penalty fees before.

\hypertarget{a.9-threat-escalation-and-decoupling}{%
\subsection{8.6a.9 Threat-Escalation and
Decoupling}\label{a.9-threat-escalation-and-decoupling}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.9 (Threat Signal).}}

At the onset of engagement with target j (t = t\_0), define the expected
vulnerability 𝒱\_expected(j,t\_0) as i's initial assessment. The threat
signal is the shortfall between that initial expectation and the
current, updated assessment:

Θ\_threat(i,j,t) = max(0, 𝒱\_expected(j,t\_0) − 𝒱(j,t)),

positive precisely when j is proving more resilient than i initially
assessed --- more aligned, or more adaptively resourceful, than
expected.

\emph{\textbf{Branching response.}} Where Θ\_threat(i,j,t)
\textgreater{} 0, i's response depends on its own position on the
self-regulation spectrum (§8.6a.5):

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Low Ψ\_total(i,t) (still fully entrenched): \textbf{escalation.}
  X\_\{i,j\}(t) gains a further multiplicative boost, (1 +
  κ\_panic·Θ\_threat(i,j,t)), increasing extraction force in direct
  proportion to how much more resistant the target has proven than
  expected --- panic, not retreat, is the entrenched predator's response
  to an unexpectedly resilient mark.
\item
  High Ψ\_total(i,t) (already drifting toward its own negentropic core):
  \textbf{decoupling.} Λ\_ij(t) is driven toward 0 over a fitted
  timescale, ending the coupling rather than escalating against it ---
  consistent with §8.6a.5's self-regulation already restraining this
  container's extraction output generally, unexpected resistance
  triggers disengagement rather than intensification.
\end{itemize}

The same threat signal, routed through the same self-regulation quantity
already governing baseline extraction intensity, produces two
structurally different outcomes depending on which side of that spectrum
the predator currently occupies --- no new decision variable is required
beyond Ψ\_total(i,t), already central to §8.6a.5.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.9a (Adaptive Escalation of Deception).}}
Where Θ\_threat(i,j,t) \textgreater{} 0 and i retains capacity to
invest, i can respond to j's resistance by escalating deception toward j
specifically rather than abandoning it. Define i's target-specific
disguise investment δ\_ij(t) ≥ 0,

dδ\_ij/dt = κ\_adapt·Θ\_threat(i,j,t)·Cap(i,t) − λ\_δ·δ\_ij(t), Cap(i,t)
= max(0, R\_i(t)/R\_i\^{}max − r\_cap,min),

which lowers the accuracy of j's perception of i (§10.5b.18.9.1),
A\_acc\^{}eff(j,i,t) = A\_acc(j,i,t)·exp(−κ\_m·δ\_ij(t)), and through it
lengthens j's hardening latency (Definition 10.5b.13b.2).

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.6a.9b (Appearance-Keyed Avoidance Loses
Ground).}} Against an extractor with Cap(i,t) \textgreater{} 0, a
defense keyed to how i appears degrades as δ\_ij grows, since it acts
through A\_acc\^{}eff. A defense keyed to realized extraction, hardening
triggered by X\_\{i,j\} itself rather than by classification of i, is
unaffected by δ\_ij, because realized flux does not depend on disguise.
The kinetic plane (Definition 10.5b.13b.4) therefore bounds realized
extraction against the most adaptive extractors only where hardening is
keyed to flux; being targeted and being susceptible remain separate
quantities.

\emph{Domain instances.} Cuckoo eggs evolve to match host eggs more
closely as hosts learn to reject them; viruses change their surface
proteins to escape immunity already built (antigenic drift); bacteria
acquire resistance to the specific antibiotic deployed against them;
phishing adapts to each generation of filters. In each, avoidance keyed
to appearance loses ground, while defenses keyed to the realized effect,
such as immune clearance of infected cells or anomaly detection on
actual transfers, do not.

\hypertarget{a.10-role-splitting-within-a-shared-sources-coupled-neighborhood}{%
\subsection{8.6a.10 Role-Splitting Within a Shared Source's Coupled
Neighborhood}\label{a.10-role-splitting-within-a-shared-sources-coupled-neighborhood}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.10 (Role-Splitting).}} A single
high-entropy source i coupled to multiple neighbors j, k within one
small sub-grid need not apply the same extraction profile to each. i may
designate j specifically for extraction (X\_\{i,j\} \textgreater{} 0,
ongoing resource drain per §8.6a) while designating k specifically for
displacement only.

Define Y\_\{i,k\}(t) ≥ 0, a direct displacement flux distinct from and
not computed from X\_\{i,k\}(t) or H\_\{i,k\}(t):

Y\_\{i,k\}(t) = χ\_Y · Λ\_ik(t) · ℐ\_i(t) / (1 + X\_\{i,j\}(t)), χ\_Y
\textgreater{} 0 fitted,

capturing displacement's real, distinct character: driven by i's own
accumulated unvented pressure (ℐ\_i(t), §8.8a.1) rather than by
extraction proceeds, and inversely related to how much extraction is
currently being profitably conducted elsewhere (against j) --- a source
with an extraction outlet already available has less need to discharge
pressure onto a second target purely for its own relief. Y\_\{i,k\}(t)
enters k's own resource dynamics as a direct cost exactly where
H\_\{i,k\}(t) would have, but is never required to satisfy H\_\{i,k\} =
(1−η\_i)X\_\{i,k\}, since k is by definition not the source of the
extraction being converted. With this separate flux, j and k retain the
real, adapter-checkable prediction already stated below: measurably
different resource trajectories despite comparable coupling strength,
since they are serving genuinely different functions for i ---
extraction proceeds for j, pure pressure discharge for k --- rather than
experiencing the same mechanism at different intensities. This is a
real, adapter-checkable prediction distinct from assuming uniform
treatment across a predator's coupled neighborhood: j and k should show
measurably different resource trajectories despite comparable coupling
strength Λ\_ij ≈ Λ\_ik to the same source, since they are serving
different functions for i rather than experiencing the same mechanism at
different intensities.

\emph{Domain instances.} A parent firm extracting from one subsidiary
while shifting liabilities onto another, the bad-bank structure; a
parasite using one host for feeding and another for transmission.

\hypertarget{b-tipping-resolution-and-momentum-discharge-the-resolution-kick}{%
\subsection{8.8b Tipping Resolution and Momentum Discharge (The
Resolution
Kick)}\label{b-tipping-resolution-and-momentum-discharge-the-resolution-kick}}

While §8.8a formalizes how high-entropy containers strike out
frantically during a cascade, a complete network propagation model must
also account for the kinetic energy discharged when a medium-entropy
container's own tipping event resolves. Once a medium-entropy container
j crosses its threshold and completes its transition, its accumulated
tipping momentum M\_tip(j) must discharge into the network, setting its
remaining coupled neighbors shaking.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8b.1 (Resolution Kick).}}

At the moment a medium-entropy container j completes a tipping event (t
= t\_tip,j), its accumulated tipping momentum M\_tip(j) is discharged
into its local network as a resolution kick K\_j(t). Where j had itself
been a parasitized target (X\_\{i,j\}(t) \textgreater{} 0 for some
coupled extractor i prior to j's own resolution, §8.6a), K\_j(t) carries
a real, further content beyond momentum alone: j's own operational
layer, genuinely intact through the resolution (§10.5b.2.13's Type B),
releases accurate signal about the extraction it actually experienced
-\/- a violent transfer of accurate information into whatever coupled
neighbor receives it, not merely a generic momentum perturbation. Where
the receiving neighbor is the extractor i itself, this is the formal
content of a decoupling target's kick reaching its own predator.

\emph{\textbf{Lemma 8.8b.1 (Impulsive vs.~Dissipative Discharge).}}

The mathematical character of K\_j(t) is determined by the tipping
resolution pathway.

\textbf{B1 (Terminal Lock):} an abrupt, non-equilibrium halt that severs
connections violently, modeled as an instantaneous impulsive strike:

\emph{K}\_j(t) = δ(t − t\_tip,j) · M\_tip(j),

where δ is the Dirac delta function.

\textbf{B2 (Reorganization):} a controlled, structural reset that
dissipates energy smoothly as the container stabilizes, modeled as a
decayed release over a characteristic dissipation window τ\_diss
\textgreater{} 0:

\emph{K}\_j(t) = (1/τ\_diss) · exp(−(t − t\_tip,j)/τ\_diss) · M\_tip(j)
· H(t − t\_tip,j),

where H(t) is the Heaviside step function. Both discharge forms
integrate to exactly M\_tip(j), so the total momentum released is
conserved regardless of which pathway resolves it --- only its time
profile differs.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.8b.1 (Momentum Propagation).}}

The resolution kick K\_j(t) propagates to each remaining coupled
neighbor k ∈ N\_j(t) as an incoming perturbation weighted by normalized
edge strength:

K\_\{j→k\}(t) = K\_j(t) · {[}w\_jk(t) / Σ\_l w\_jl(t){]}, where Σ\_l
w\_jl(t) \textgreater{} 0 -\/- w\_jk(t) the scalar edge weight of §8.7,
distinct from the interaction tuple of §8.2.

\emph{\textbf{Units, stated explicitly.}} K\_j(t) and its routed shares
K\_\{j→k\}(t) are abstract scalar impulses --- the conserved quantity is
Σ\_k K\_\{j→k\}(t) = K\_j(t), a scalar sum, not a physical vector
momentum in any Newtonian sense. Calling this a ``perturbation vector''
elsewhere in this document refers to its role as an input vector across
multiple receiving neighbors simultaneously, not to vector-valued
conservation in the physical sense; weighted scalar partitioning
conserves the scalar total exactly, which is the only conservation claim
made here.

Solitary-container clause. Where Σ\_l w\_jl(t) = 0 --- a tipping
container with no remaining coupled neighbors --- the routing formula
above is undefined and no network-routing term is computed. The
resolution kick is dissipated entirely internally, through the
container's own boundary dynamics (§7.4's balance equation), rather than
distributed to a nonexistent neighbor set.

\textbf{Dynamical coupling.}

This momentum transfer acts as the exogenous shock required for regime
genesis in §10.5b.2. Three points need to be made explicit for this to
be a complete mechanism rather than a partially-specified one.

\textbf{Routing through the balance equation.} K\_\{j→k\}(t) enters
neighbor k's dynamics as a shock term within §7.4's balance equation ---
concretely, as a transient negative contribution to E\_exch,k(t) at the
moment of arrival --- rather than being written directly into δR\_k(t).
§7.9 defines δR as an integral over the balance equation's terms; a
direct write would bypass that derivation and leave two independent
mechanisms populating the same integral, which §7.9's formula does not
support. Routed this way, the kick's effect on δR\_k(t) is exactly what
§7.9's integral already computes once E\_exch,k(t) reflects the shock,
with no separate update rule required.

\textbf{Sourcing the verticality signal.} §10.5b.2's branching logic
requires both S\_shock(t) and an ambient signal v\_gen(t) to determine
which regime a shocked baseline container branches into; K\_\{j→k\}(t)
supplies the former but not, by itself, the latter. The natural source,
consistent with §10.5b.2's ``sourced directly from the container that
inflicted the shock,'' is j's own resolved outcome: a B1
(terminal-locked) resolution transmits a high V(t) alongside its kick
--- consistent with a locked container being a source of rigid,
closed-circuit corruption --- while a B2 (reorganized) resolution
transmits a low V(t), consistent with a reorganizing container radiating
recovered, open signal rather than corrupted signal. This detail is
required for K\_\{j→k\}(t) to actually drive §10.5b.2's branching rather
than supplying only half of what that mechanism needs.

\textbf{Neighbors already in a non-baseline regime.} §10.5b.2's
branching logic is stated for a previously low-entropy (baseline)
container. Where a receiving neighbor k is already high- or
medium-entropy, a resolution kick does not re-trigger branching; it
instead adds to k's existing S\_shock accumulation or Drive\_B forcing
(§10.5b.6--§8.8a.iii, as appropriate to k's current regime), consistent
with k already having a regime-appropriate dynamic that the kick
perturbs rather than restarts.

This closes the network-wide dynamical cascade loop, allowing a tipping
event in one sector to systematically trigger regime-branching in
adjacent baseline containers, and to perturb already-classified
neighbors consistently with their existing dynamics.

\emph{\textbf{Local genesis within new sub-structure, and revised core
reclassification.}} An earlier statement of this rule held that an
already-classified container's overall regime is \emph{never} reopened
by later exposure. That was too strong, and is corrected here: a mature
container's core regime can shift, but the force required to do it ---
magnitude and duration together, the same v\_gen(t)-style quantity
driving original genesis --- scales with how much the container has
already structurally consolidated. A newly-forming container has little
accumulated structure to overcome, so ordinary developmental exposure is
sufficient to set its trajectory; a mature container has real
accumulated inertia, so reopening its core classification requires
something proportionally stronger and more sustained than an ordinary
passing interaction --- sustained, concentrated conditions such as
prolonged fame (sustained false negentropic feedback from across a grid)
or prolonged societal vilification (sustained false entropic feedback
from across a grid), rather than any single ordinary exposure. This is a
threshold condition, not a prohibition: what the original rule was
actually protecting against --- an unremarkable later interaction
casually overturning a settled classification, which would make regime
meaningless as a diagnostic --- remains fully protected, since ordinary
exposure essentially never crosses a mature container's much higher
threshold.

Separately from this threshold-scaled core reclassification, a mature
container may also form genuinely new recursive sub-structure after its
own formative window has closed --- a new sustained membership, a new
role, a new coupling maintained long enough to constitute its own
developing sub-container rather than a passing interaction. That new
sub-structure undergoes its own genesis-branching event, locally, per
Definition 10.5b.2.1, independent of the parent container's own core
classification and its own, separately-scaled threshold. A container
with a stable, negentropic formative history that later joins a
predatory organization does not have its own prior classification
rewritten by that membership alone; the membership itself, as new
sub-structure, is what branches high- or medium-entropy on v\_gen(t)'s
ordinary terms, sitting alongside whatever the container already was.
These are two distinct mechanisms --- one raising the bar for reopening
the core classification itself, the other opening a separate, local
branching process for new sub-structure --- not competing accounts of
the same thing.

\textbf{8.9.1 Common-Cause Detection Without Source Instrumentation}

\emph{\textbf{Motivation.}} §8.6a's H\_i(t) already formalizes lateral
knocks --- unmetabolized failure from a source venting onto coupled
neighbors --- but using it presupposes the source is already known and
its own internal state (self-deception, extraction dynamics) is
measurable. This is often not available in practice: a shared
higher-order source (a funding body, a regulatory regime, a market-wide
shock) may sit several recursive levels above the containers actually
being tracked, genuinely unmeasurable by the adapter in hand, while its
effects on multiple otherwise-unrelated children remain directly
observable. What follows detects a shared external forcing term from the
children's own behavior alone, without instrumenting the source at all.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.9.1 (Common-Cause Signature).}} For a
population of N containers not directly coupled to each other (no Λ\_ij
edges among them, or only weak/indirect ones) but sharing dependence on
a common higher-order source --- siblings under the same recursive
parent, per §5.2, whether or not that parent is itself being tracked ---
define the co-movement statistic

Co(t) = (number of children with Δ(observable\_i)(t) \textgreater{}
θ\_shift) / N,

for a shared observable already tracked per-child (δR(t), D\_op(t), or
another adapter-supplied quantity), θ\_shift a fitted per-child shift
threshold. Co(t) is compared against Co\_baseline, the rate of
simultaneous shifts expected under independent, uncorrelated individual
dynamics (estimated from the same population's own history, or
adapter-supplied). Where Co(t) ≫ Co\_baseline at a given t, this
constitutes a Common-Cause Signature: statistically unusual
synchronization across children with no direct coupling among themselves
is evidence consistent with a shared external forcing term acting on all
of them, regardless of whether that source's own internal state is ever
measured. It screens for a common cause rather than establishing one,
since shared seasonality, common measurement artifacts, indirect
coupling and several simultaneous causes can produce the same
synchronization. The shift observable is standardized so that its
positive direction is degradation for every child (for example −Δd\_A
alongside +ΔδR).

\emph{\textbf{Consequence for collapse-risk interpretation.}} A
confirmed Common-Cause Signature changes what a given child's own
degrading signals should be read as evidence of. Absorbing a shared
external shock alongside statistically many peers is a genuinely
different diagnostic category from independently generated internal
self-deception or extraction dynamics, even though both can produce
identical-looking δR(t) or D\_op(t) trajectories in isolation. During a
confirmed common-cause episode, P\_C(t) (§11.10) should be flagged as
reflecting shared, externally-forced strain rather than treated
identically to isolated internal decline --- the same discipline already
required of the divergent-signal flag (§11.10.1): a score that cannot
distinguish two different causal stories should say so, not silently
average them into one number that answers neither question.

\hypertarget{part-9.-memory-dynamics}{%
\section{Part 9. Memory Dynamics}\label{part-9.-memory-dynamics}}

\hypertarget{memory-geometry-and-identity}{%
\subsection{9.10 Memory Geometry and
Identity}\label{memory-geometry-and-identity}}

Definition 4.8 (§4.10) establishes that the informational distance a
container experiences is itself history-dependent: g\_t = g(M\_t), with
the velocity, acceleration, curvature, and geodesic constructions of
§4.3--§4.6 generalizing accordingly. The consequence for memory
specifically is this: two systems occupying identical instantaneous
informational states may nonetheless experience fundamentally different
adaptive trajectories, because their historical geometries --- encoded
through M\_t --- differ. A container with a long history of successful
correction and one with a long history of unaddressed divergence are not
the same system merely because they happen to coincide at a given P, and
g\_t is what allows that difference to enter the mathematics rather than
remaining a narrative aside.

This raises a question Parts 2 through 8 do not answer: what makes a
container, tracked across an extended history during which its realized
boundary, resource level, and even its recursive membership may all have
changed, the same container throughout? Definition 2.2 (as revised)
gives a container C a fixed identity, (Ω, B̂\_C, M̂\_C), distinct from its
evolving realized state X\_t = (P\_t, R\_t, B\_t, M\_t) --- so formal
identity, in the narrow sense of ``possesses the same generating
schemas,'' is already well-defined and does not itself present a
circularity or persistence problem. But that formal notion is weaker
than what this section needs: two containers could share identical
schemas without being the same real-world entity under study, and,
conversely, a real system's own boundary-generating or memory-generating
schema can itself gradually change (through the ordinary operation of
aggregation, §5.2, or sustained structural adaptation) without the
system thereby becoming ``a different container'' in any sense an
application would recognize. Formal schema identity is therefore not
what this section means by identity; something else must ground it.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 9.2 (Recursive Membership Continuity).}}

Let a container C's recursive membership at time t, μ(C,t), denote the
set of lower-level containers currently aggregated into C via the
operator A of Definition 5.1. For an interval {[}t′, t{]}, define the
membership-continuity functional

\emph{χ}(t, t′) = {[}Σ\_\{i ∈ μ(C,t)∩μ(C,t′)\} R\_i(t′){]} / {[}Σ\_\{i ∈
μ(C,t′)\} R\_i(t′){]},

Both sums use the same reference weights, the members' resources at the
start of the interval, which guarantees χ ∈ {[}0,1{]}. Where the
denominator is zero, χ is undefined and membership continuity is
assessed by count instead, χ\_count(t,t′) = \textbar μ(C,t) ∩
μ(C,t′)\textbar{} / \textbar μ(C,t′)\textbar.

the resource-weighted (per §5.2's pooling weights) fraction of C's
membership at t′ that remains part of C's membership at t. χ ∈
{[}0,1{]}; χ = 1 indicates unchanged membership, χ = 0 indicates
complete membership turnover.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 9.3 (Identity Persistence, memory-continuity
component).}}

A container C is said to persist as one continuous entity across {[}t′,
t{]} if χ(t, t′) remains above a domain-specified threshold χ\_min
throughout the interval. This criterion is genealogical rather than
substantive: it tracks continuity of clustering, not constancy of any
particular structural property. A container may shed and regenerate a
large share of its lower-level membership over a long enough interval
--- as, for example, a physical structure gradually loses its original
constituent members while remaining recognizably one bounded entity
across any shorter timescale --- and still satisfy Definition 9.3 at
every shorter sub-interval, provided membership turnover in any given
window stays below the threshold. Falling below χ\_min does not by
itself indicate an ending in a normative sense; it indicates that the
entity being tracked has changed to a degree the observer's threshold
treats as a different entity, which is a modeling choice, not a fact
about the system independent of that choice.

Definition 9.3 gives only the memory-side half of container identity.
Temporal clustering alone does not distinguish a bounded container from
a diffuse, unbounded region of the same underlying field --- some
minimum structural coherence is also required, or χ could remain high
for a cluster that was never really a bounded entity in the first place.
§10.5b.0 completes the criterion once the semi-rigidity condition (h(t)
≥ h\_min) is formally available, and states there why full identity
requires both conditions jointly, and how the two conditions together
distinguish genuine dissolution from the structural relaxation described
in that section's account of Ultimate Convergence.

\hypertarget{internal-cohesion-as-the-mechanism-of-continuity}{%
\subsection{9.10.1 Internal Cohesion as the Mechanism of
Continuity}\label{internal-cohesion-as-the-mechanism-of-continuity}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 9.10.1 (Internal Cohesion).}}

For container C with recursive membership μ(C,t) (Definition 9.2),
define the internal cohesion measure as the resource-weighted mean of
§8.5's existing pairwise coupling strength Λ\_ij, restricted to pairs
currently co-resident in C's own membership:

Λ\_int(C,t) = {[}Σ\_\{i,j ∈ μ(C,t), i≠j\} R\_i·R\_j·Λ\_ij(t){]} /
{[}Σ\_\{i,j ∈ μ(C,t), i≠j\} R\_i·R\_j{]}.

This introduces no new primitive; it is Λ\_ij, already defined for
exactly this purpose in §8.5, aggregated over a container's own current
sub-members rather than between two containers external to each other.

\emph{\textbf{Null-membership convention.}} For a container C with no
recursive membership (μ(C,t) = ∅), Λ\_int(C,t) is defined as 0 for all
computational purposes, rather than left undefined. This ensures
numerical continuity in §8.3.1's first-contact attraction weight
A\_first = ν\_pref·Λ\_int, where a solitary candidate correctly
contributes zero attraction rather than producing an undefined or null
value the engine cannot propagate.

The same convention applies whenever the denominator of Λ\_int is zero,
including a singleton membership and a membership whose members all have
zero resource weight: Λ\_int(C,t) = 0.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 9.10.1 (Cohesion Sustains Continuity).}}

A member i ∈ μ(C,t') remains in μ(C,t) at a later time --- contributing
to χ(t,t') remaining above χ\_min rather than to membership turnover ---
in proportion to i's coupling strength to the rest of C's current
membership relative to i's coupling strength to entities outside C.
Formally, membership retention for i is increasing in Λ\_ij(t) summed
over j ∈ μ(C,t)\{i\} and decreasing in Λ\_ik(t) summed over k ∉ μ(C,t).
This gives Definition 9.3's asserted criterion an actual mechanism:
χ(t,t') stays high not because membership is stipulated to persist, but
because internal coupling among a container's own current members is, on
net, stronger than whatever external coupling would pull individual
members away.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 9.10.1.1 (Cohesion Is the Sole Containment
Mechanism at the Floor).}}

At the semi-rigid matrix state (V(t) = h\_min, χ\_supp = 1, per
§10.5b.0.4), vertical containment contributes nothing to boundedness ---
there is no wall left to do that work. Where a container at this state
nonetheless satisfies Definition 9.3 (χ(t,t') ≥ χ\_min), Proposition
9.10.1 identifies Λ\_int(C,t) as the entire explanation: internal
cohesion is not one contributor to containment alongside structural
rigidity at the floor, it is the only one available. A container
occupying the semi-rigid matrix state with low Λ\_int(C,t) should
therefore show elevated membership turnover and be at genuine risk of
falling below χ\_min --- boundedness at minimal verticality is not
automatic, it is earned continuously by the container's own internal
coupling density, with nothing in reserve to fall back on if that
density drops.

\hypertarget{adaptive-forgetting}{%
\subsection{9.11 Adaptive Forgetting}\label{adaptive-forgetting}}

Define the forgetting operator F\_M : M\_t → M\_t′. Adaptive forgetting
removes informational detail whose maintenance costs exceed its future
adaptive value. Both excessive forgetting and excessive retention can
impair adaptive performance.

\textbf{The Memory Principle.}

Memory is an active operator that compresses, reconstructs, weights, and
transforms historical experience, thereby modifying the geometry of
future adaptation across every recursive level of organization --- and,
through the continuity of that historical organization, constitutes a
container's persisting identity across the transformations the rest of
this framework allows it to undergo.

\hypertarget{part-10.-adaptive-stability-and-collapse}{%
\section{Part 10. Adaptive Stability and
Collapse}\label{part-10.-adaptive-stability-and-collapse}}

\hypertarget{b.0-the-topology-of-verticality-and-horizontality}{%
\subsection{10.5b.0 The Topology of Verticality and
Horizontality}\label{b.0-the-topology-of-verticality-and-horizontality}}

The entropy regime classification developed in this section
distinguishes containers by the relationship between their official and
operational layers. That classification describes symptoms, diagnosed
from observable variance. The present subsection supplies a structural
account of what those symptoms are symptoms of: why containers assume
rigid, closed, extractive configurations or fluid, open, negentropic
ones, and why one configuration is dramatically more durable than the
other.

\textbf{Why some rigidity is necessary, and why excess rigidity is
costly.}

A container with no structural rigidity whatsoever is not a bounded
entity; it is indistinguishable from the undifferentiated field around
it. Some minimum degree of structural coherence is therefore
constitutive of being a container at all --- not an unfortunate
compromise, but a precondition for Axiom 1 to apply. At the same time,
rigidity beyond that minimum is costly: a container that over-invests in
rigid, closed, hierarchical structure decays faster than one that
maintains only the minimum coherence its informational alignment
actually requires. The pattern recurs across scales and domains --- tall
physical structures under greater structural strain than broad ones of
comparable mass; large-bodied organisms carrying disproportionate
maintenance load for their size (already formalized as a scale effect on
maintenance cost, §7.5); rigid institutional hierarchies that fracture
under stress that flatter, more distributed organizations absorb. The
formal account below treats structural height as the dependent variable
in this relationship and informational alignment as the driver, rather
than treating the two as independent quantities that happen to
correlate.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom V1 (Verticality Is Rigid).}} These are named V1/V2,
distinct from Part 3's foundational Axiom 1/Axiom 2 and from the
collapse-layer's Axiom C1--C4 (§10.5b.15), to avoid colliding with
either --- the verticality-layer's own foundational claims, stated here
explicitly for the first time despite grounding material (h(t), V(t),
the pancaking Directional Alignment Effect of §8.8a.ix) that has
depended on them since RICD 10.3. A container under sufficient stress or
velocity concentrates its own energy into rigid, closed structure rather
than remaining open and distributed --- form, in this document's sense,
hardening as a direct consequence of pressure rather than as an
independent design choice.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom V2 (Horizontality Is Fluid).}} The complementary
claim: absent that concentrating pressure, a container's natural state
is open, distributed, uncompressed exchange --- function operating
freely across the container's own structure rather than being channeled
through a rigid form. V1 and V2 together are the reason h(t) is defined
as relaxing toward h\_target(I(t)) rather than being independently
fitted (Definition 10.5b.0.1 immediately below): rigidity is not a free
parameter, it is what V1 says happens under pressure, and fluidity is
what V2 says is recovered once that pressure lifts.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.1 (Structural Height and the
Verticality Operator).}}

Let a container C have a normalized structural height h(t) ∈ {[}h\_min,
1{]}, where h\_min \textgreater{} 0 is a strictly positive floor --- the
minimum structural coherence consistent with C remaining a bounded
container at all (§2.2, Axiom 1) --- and h(t) = 1 represents maximal,
closed, hierarchical rigidity. This is not a physical height in every
domain; it is a structural index of how rigid, closed, and
hierarchically constrained the container's boundary currently is.

Rather than treating h(t) as an independently fitted quantity, h(t) is
driven causally by informational alignment I(t) (§6.6) through a
relaxation dynamic:

d\emph{h}/dt = κ\_h · {[} h\_target(I(t)) − h(t) {]}, h\_target(I) =
h\_min + (1 − h\_min)·(1 − I)\^{}ξ,

with κ\_h \textgreater{} 0 a fitted relaxation rate and ξ ≥ 1 a fitted
exponent. Higher alignment sets a lower structural-height target; lower
alignment sets a higher one. Because h(t) chases h\_target(I(t)) rather
than jumping to it, structural relaxation necessarily lags behind and
follows alignment improvement --- it does not lead it, and cannot
produce a self-sustaining reduction in structural height by any
mechanism that intervenes on h(t) directly without I(t) itself
improving: a direct intervention can lower h(t) for as long as it
continues, but h(t) resumes relaxing toward h\_target(I(t)) the moment
that forcing lapses, per the same equation. The verticality operator is
then simply:

\emph{Status of the functional form.} This relaxation equation is a
chosen functional form, not a result derived from Parts 4--7: the
direction of causation from alignment to structural height, and the
specific relaxation dynamics, are model-dependent hypotheses tested by
the predictions stated for Axioms V1 and V2. Cross-domain use of h(t)
and χ\_supp also requires an adapter to show that its measures of
structural height and support are comparable across the systems
compared, rather than assuming it.

\emph{V}(t) := h(t) ∈ {[}h\_min, 1{]}.

This replaces a bare functional Φ\_V (monotonic in dh/dt, d²h/dt², S,
δR, and Π\_f) with a computable form, by observing that once h(t) is
itself derived from I(t) --- which is already a function of S, δR, and
Π\_f's downstream effects on D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm through the dynamics of
Part 6 --- a separate wrapper functional adds complexity without adding
information. The rate-sensitivity the original formulation wanted
(verticality responding to how fast a container is rigidifying, not
merely how rigid it currently is) is preserved implicitly: h(t)'s own
relaxation dynamic already responds continuously to changes in I(t), so
dh/dt is recoverable directly from the equation above without a separate
operator.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary (Alignment Precedes Relaxation; a falsifiable
prediction).}}

Because h(t) chases h\_target(I(t)) with lag rather than tracking it
exactly, two directly testable predictions follow from the single
equation above, without further assumption. First, sustained improvement
in I(t) should measurably precede a corresponding decline in h(t), not
the reverse. Second, any intervention that forces h(t) below its
currently-implied target --- flattening structure without underlying
alignment actually improving --- predicts its own reversal: since dh/dt
= κ\_h·{[}h\_target(I(t)) − h(t){]} is positive whenever h(t) is below
target, the same dynamic that governs ordinary relaxation drives an
artificially-flattened container's structural height back up toward
h\_target(I(t)) once the intervention lapses. This is the formal
counterpart to the observation that forcing apparent flatness without
genuine alignment tends to produce rebound rigidity rather than lasting
change: it is not a separate claim requiring its own mechanism, but a
direct consequence of treating alignment as causally prior to structural
relaxation.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.2 (Horizontality Operator).}}

Horizontality is the dual of verticality, not an independently fitted
quantity:

\emph{𝒲}(t) := 1 − V(t) = 1 − h(t) ∈ {[}0, 1−h\_min{]}.

Because h\_min \textgreater{} 0, 𝒲(t) never reaches 1: a container never
achieves complete, unconstrained fluidity, consistent with some minimum
structural coherence being constitutive of remaining a bounded container
at all (Definition 10.5b.0.1). Maximal horizontality is 1 − h\_min ---
the semi-rigid matrix state --- not a fully flat, structureless field.
Proposition 10.5b.0.4a (h\_min Is Substrate-Dependent, Not Universal).
h\_min's own value is not a single constant across all containers; it is
set by what a container's own boundary type (§2.10) requires to remain
bounded at all. A material container's boundary is endogenously
precipitated by its own internal physiology (§2.10's own distinction)
and therefore carries a genuinely higher h\_min floor than a conceptual
container's: a material container below some substrate-specific minimum
structural rigidity is not a healthier instance of that container, it is
a materially dissolved one, whereas a conceptual container's boundary,
sustained purely by active relational exchange among messengers rather
than by physiology, carries no equivalent physical requirement and can
approach a genuinely lower h\_min. This gives real, structural content
to the intuition that no single material container, however healthy, can
itself instantiate the semi-rigid horizontal matrix state in its fullest
sense -\/- individuation into a bounded material entity at all already
costs some minimum verticality no amount of internal alignment relaxes
away -\/- while a sufficiently coupled, aligned network of such
containers (§8.5's Λ\_ij at high, stable values across the whole grid)
is not bound by any single member's own material floor, and can
collectively approach a lower effective h\_min than any one of its
material members could individually reach. A tidal marsh or seagrass
meadow's own characteristic longevity, relative to the individual
organisms composing it, is the natural instance of this: the
network-level container genuinely outlives what its own material
constituents' individual floors would predict.

Proposition 10.5b.0.4b (Separate Coupled Systems Required, Not Merely
Internal Complexity). The lowered effective h\_min above requires
genuine multiplicity of separate, externally-coupled containers -\/- it
is not available to a single container merely by virtue of possessing
rich internal structure or many internal sub-parts. A single container,
however elaborate its own internal organization, remains one bounded
thing at the level Definition 2.2 individuates it, and is subject to the
same individuation-cost logic as any other single material container
regardless of how complex its interior is. The marsh outlives its
individual organisms specifically because it is composed of genuinely
separate, externally-coupled organisms, each its own container joined by
real Λ\_ij coupling (§8.5) -\/- not because it is one large organism
with many internal parts. A single system cannot lower its own effective
h\_min by growing more internally elaborate; only coupling to genuinely
separate others accomplishes this.

\textbf{Geometric support ratio.}

Height and rigidity alone do not determine structural durability. Two
containers with identical h(t) can differ sharply in how long that
structure persists, depending on how broadly the structure's boundary is
grounded relative to its vertical extension --- a broad-based structure
distributes load across a wide foundation; a narrow one concentrates it.
The same distinction separates a broad-based, widely distributed
structure from a narrow, concentrated one of comparable height, or a
broadly stakeholder-supported institution from a narrowly hierarchical
one of comparable size.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.3 (Geometric Support Ratio).}}

Define χ\_supp(C,t) ∈ (0,1{]}, a domain-specified measure of the breadth
of a container's structural or organizational base relative to its
vertical extension (adapter-supplied --- e.g.~base-contact area relative
to height for a physical structure, or breadth of distributed
stakeholder support relative to hierarchical depth for an institution).
χ\_supp = 1 represents maximal support for the container's current
height; values near 0 represent a narrow, precarious base relative to
height.

\textbf{Structural hazard and longevity.}

Rigidity beyond the minimum, and inadequate support for whatever
rigidity is present, both shorten a container's expected structural
lifespan. This is the formal content of the claim that vertical,
high-divergence structures burn themselves out faster than semi-rigid,
well-supported ones.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.4 (Structural Hazard Rate).}}

\emph{Ω}\_hazard(t) = κ\_Ω · V(t)\^{}ψ / χ\_supp(C,t),

where ψ ≥ 1 is a fitted exponent (ψ \textgreater{} 1 giving a
superlinear penalty for excess verticality, consistent with the
scale-dependence already established for maintenance cost in §7.5) and
κ\_Ω \textgreater{} 0 is a fitted rate coefficient. Hazard is minimized
--- but never driven to zero --- when V(t) = h\_min and χ\_supp = 1: the
semi-rigid matrix state, maximally supported, is the longest-lived
configuration a container can occupy, not an immortal one. Ω\_hazard(t)
\textgreater{} 0 always, since h\_min \textgreater{} 0; every container
remains subject to nonzero structural dissolution risk indefinitely,
consistent with no container being permanently exempt from Axiom 3's
nonzero cost of informational processing. Positive hazard alone does
not, by itself, guarantee eventual dissolution with certainty --- a
hazard rate that declines quickly enough can yield a finite cumulative
hazard and therefore a genuinely positive probability of indefinite
survival; the stronger claim of certain eventual dissolution
additionally requires the cumulative hazard to diverge, ∫₀\^{}∞
Ω\_hazard(t) dt = ∞, which this document does not assume by default and
