launch threshold (§8.8a) over time. A single sufficiently large Drive\_B
can still cross that threshold alone, and genuinely catastrophic
single-shock collapse remains real and already covered by the existing
threshold condition; the claim here is comparative, not exclusive: the
cumulative case is the common one, the singular-huge-shock case the real
but less frequent exception.

Definition 10.5b.9f.4 (Medium-Entropy's Defining Dual Property). What
distinguishes medium-entropy from either pure extreme is not the absence
of vulnerability but its coexistence with real resilience: a
medium-entropy container carries both a genuine susceptibility to
further knocks and a genuine, functioning capacity to reset (§10.5b.9's
B1/B2 branch point). Neither property alone is definitional; the regime
is constituted by holding both simultaneously.

Proposition 10.5b.9f.5 (Resilience Tends to Win Over Time, Not by
Chance). Where a container repeatedly survives its own
vulnerability-resilience tension via successful B2 cycles, the balance
does not remain fixed at whatever ratio originally obtained. Repeated
successful cycling (§10.5b.9a.3-.4) combines with the unforced
negentropic default already established at Axiom C1 to produce a
systematic drift toward resilience and away from the
originally-conferred vulnerability, even from an initially
equal-weighted starting point -\/- directional, not the product of
chance recovery.

Proposition 10.5b.9f.6 (High-Entropy's Low Resilience, Misrecognized as
Armor). High-entropy containers are, by this same measure, genuinely low
in resilience -\/- their own operational corruption is itself evidence
of a resilience deficit rather than a compensating strength. The rigid,
vertical structure a high-entropy container builds around itself
(§10.5b.0) is characteristically misrecognized by the container's own
operational layer as protective armor, when it is structurally a symptom
of the same low resilience the container is failing to correct, not a
genuine defense against it.

Proposition 10.5b.9f.7 (The Newly-Forming System's Double
Vulnerability). A container with no history yet has, by 10.5b.9f.4-.5's
own logic, neither the resilience nor the accumulated learning a mature
container has had the chance to build -\/- it has no established
trajectory at all, in either direction. This is precisely why the
specific combination of a developmental knock together with inaccurate
negentropic feedback (from the knocking party or the surrounding grid,
§10.5b.9a.3c) is uniquely potent for a newly-forming system: with no
prior trajectory to resist reorientation, that combination is what sets
verticality as the system's own original trajectory, corrupting its
operational layer from the point of origin rather than displacing an
already-established healthy one.

Proposition 10.5b.9f.8 (The Maturity Asymmetry, Following From the
Above). A mature container, having built real resilience through
10.5b.9f.5's own mechanism, requires a genuinely large shock -\/- or
sustained, inescapable relentless knocking within a hostile grid it
cannot exit -\/- to change its established orientation. A newly-forming
container, per 10.5b.9f.7, can be oriented by comparatively minor
events, since there is no built resilience yet to overcome. This
asymmetry is not a property of the memory kernel's own update
mathematics in isolation; it follows from the resilience a mature system
has already accumulated by the time a new shock arrives, per 10.5b.9f.1
and 10.5b.9f.5 taken together.

\textbf{10.5b.10 Steadying vs.~One-Time Reset}

A medium-entropy container that completes a B2 reorganization, or is
steadied pre-flip, benefits from external anchoring primarily as a speed
multiplier. A high-entropy container is structurally different: merely
righting or resetting it without addressing internal composition is
insufficient, since its unaltered internal mechanics (F\_i, still driven
by whatever unresolved D\_op and unvented heat remain) will trigger
renewed violent oscillation almost immediately. What this regime
requires is sustained steadying --- continuous external containment
disallowing both further oscillation and further extraction/displacement
(§8.6a) --- maintained over time, not applied once. Sustained steadying
is also the mechanism by which a high-entropy container's composition
can gradually shift toward the medium-entropy or low-entropy baseline
regimes, per Axiom C1: with extraction and oscillation both disallowed
for long enough, the feedback operator loses the external support it
needs to sustain convergence around the corrupted signal, and drifts
toward the true one instead.

\hypertarget{b.11a-post-breach-redirection-versus-resistance}{%
\subsection{10.5b.11a Post-Breach Redirection versus
Resistance}\label{b.11a-post-breach-redirection-versus-resistance}}

Once a container has crossed hull-breach (§10.5a) and entered runaway
acceleration (Axiom C2), the character of external intervention needed
reverses from what §10.5b.10's prevention guidance recommends. Before
hull-breach, opposing an oscillation's phase is damping and protective
(§10.5b.6). After hull-breach, the same opposing intervention is
destructive: momentum that has already accumulated cannot be canceled,
only redirected or blocked, and blocking forces a violent, concentrated
discharge rather than preventing one.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11a.1 (Redirection Channel).}}

At the moment a container's tipping momentum M\_tip(t) is discharging
(§8.8b), define a redirection channel availability Γ\_redir(t) ∈
{[}0,1{]}: the degree to which an external structure --- an anchor, an
aligned momentum source, or a coupled neighbor --- is positioned to
carry the discharging momentum through a full rotation rather than
absorb or oppose it. Γ\_redir(t) = 0 indicates no such structure is
available or engaged; Γ\_redir(t) = 1 indicates full redirection
capacity.

\emph{\textbf{Computational default.}} Where no domain-specific
composition is supplied, Γ\_redir(t) = max(Γ\_anchor(t), Γ\_neighbor(t),
Γ\_intrinsic(t)), the largest of: anchor-supplied availability
(Anchor(k→C,t), §10.5b.2.6, where present), the strongest
currently-aligned coupled neighbor's own capacity to receive redirected
momentum, and the intrinsic fluid-mass-derived channel already given for
Flip Condition B below. Taking the maximum rather than a sum or average
reflects that a single sufficient channel, not the accumulation of
several partial ones, is what carries a full rotation; this default is
adapter-overridable where a domain-specific composition is better
justified.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11a.1 (Discharge Character
Generalized).}}

Lemma 8.8b.1's impulsive-versus-dissipative discharge split, previously
determined by which outcome (B1 or B2) a container reached, is a special
case of a more general rule: discharge is impulsive when Γ\_redir(t)
\textless{} Θ\_redir during the tipping window, and dissipative when
Γ\_redir(t) ≥ Θ\_redir, Θ\_redir ∈ (0,1) fitted or normalized to the
same scale as this document's other outcome thresholds --- an explicit,
evaluable threshold replacing the earlier qualitative ``sufficiently
high,'' regardless of route. For Flip Condition B, fluid-mass weighting
(§10.5b.7) is what determines Γ\_redir intrinsically --- μ\_p
\textgreater{} μ\_o gives B2 an inherent channel B1 lacks. For Flip
Condition A, no route supplies Γ\_redir intrinsically (Corollary
10.5b.0.1 holds unmodified for the unaided case); a nonzero Γ\_redir
must be supplied externally.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11a.2 (Resistance Blocks the Channel).}}

An intervention that opposes momentum during active discharge (t near
t\_tip, §8.8b) rather than redirecting it drives Γ\_redir(t) → 0
regardless of what Γ\_redir would otherwise have been, forcing the
impulsive discharge form even where a channel was structurally
available. This is the formal content of bracing being harmful
specifically after hull-breach: resistance does not reduce M\_tip(t), it
removes the channel through which M\_tip(t) could otherwise discharge
gradually.

\textbf{Two of three routes to nonzero Γ\_redir for Flip Condition A
(the third, involving a second struggling container, is developed
separately in §10.5b.11b).}

\textbf{1. External anchor (A1 or A2).} An anchor engaged during the
discharge window (§10.5b.13's eligibility conditions apply) supplies
Γ\_redir directly, converting the container's governing dynamics for
that window from F\_i-dominated to effectively Σ\_jΛ\_ij-dominated ---
lending the container Flip B's full-rotation option for the duration of
the intervention. This is the general-purpose route and the only one
available to a purely self-propelled (A1) container, which has no
external component to redirect in the first place.

\textbf{2. Aligned external knock (A2 only).} Where the tipping event is
already externally triggered (A2's own definition) and the knock's
direction aligns with D\_om → 0 convergence, the knock itself supplies
Γ\_redir without requiring a separately engaged anchor. This route is
unavailable to A1, which by definition has no external strike to align.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 10.5b.11a.1 (Stripped Reorganization).}}

Route 2 reaches the same M̃\_tip ≥ M\_c threshold band as B2 (§10.5b.9),
but the resulting reorganization is not B2's organic convergence around
a validated operational signal. Because the redirecting force was an
external strike rather than the container's own accurately-monitored
operational layer, the reset carries the operational layer to a null or
rebuilt state rather than recovering it --- a full rotation is
completed, S(t) and δR(t) reset, but the container's operational history
is discarded rather than preserved. This is a survivable, non-terminal
outcome, and a costly one, distinct from both terminal lock and clean
reorganization.

A third route --- mutual coupling with another struggling container ---
also supplies Γ\_redir, but involves enough additional structure
(predatory targeting, accidental capture, and voluntary sacrifice are
all distinct sub-cases) to warrant its own subsection: see §10.5b.11b.

Falsifiable prediction. Consistent with Axiom C2's evidentiary framing:
containers with an engaged redirection channel during discharge should
show measurably longer, lower-magnitude discharge signatures
(dissipative-form K\_j(t)) and higher post-event survival/reorganization
rates than matched containers whose channel is blocked or absent during
the same discharge window, holding M\_tip(t) itself constant.

\hypertarget{b.11b-mutual-coupling-under-collapse-predation-accident-and-sacrifice}{%
\subsection{10.5b.11b Mutual Coupling Under Collapse: Predation,
Accident, and
Sacrifice}\label{b.11b-mutual-coupling-under-collapse-predation-accident-and-sacrifice}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11b.1 (Discharge Alignment Tolerance).}}

Two containers' discharges qualify as coupled --- for any of the
sub-cases below --- if t\_tip,A − t\_tip,B \textless{} ε\_align (a
fitted temporal tolerance) and their forcing direction vectors satisfy
û\_A·û\_B \textgreater{} cos(θ\_align) for a fitted angular tolerance
θ\_align. This gives every sub-case a genuine, nonzero-probability basin
rather than requiring exact coincidence.

\textbf{i. Predatory Targeting (Deliberate).}

A high-entropy container approaching terminal lock (M̃\_tip(i)
approaching the M\_lock/M\_c band, §10.5b.9) may extend its targeting
beyond its ordinary extraction pattern --- §8.6a's ρ(j), which
structurally favors medium-entropy neighbors and disfavors baseline ones
--- to include an otherwise-untargeted low-entropy baseline neighbor,
using that neighbor's structure as a landing surface for its own
discharge. Two mechanisms, both operative and not mutually exclusive:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Gradual aperture widening. ρ(j) gains an explicit dependence on the
  extracting container's own proximity to terminal lock: ρ(j;i) =
  1/\{1+exp{[}−(ρ\_0(j) + κ\_desp·F̄\_i(t)){]}\} (§8.6a.1's corrected,
  bounded form; F̄\_i(t) = 1−exp(−λ\_F·ℱ\_i(t))) --- as i's own
  accumulated unvented heat rises, its targeting discrimination
  continuously loosens to include neighbors it would ordinarily ignore.
\item
  Switched pathway. Independent of the above, once i crosses into the
  terminal-lock band, a separate targeting mode --- off below the band,
  on above it --- activates, reflecting a genuinely different behavioral
  mode rather than a continuation of ordinary extraction.
\end{itemize}

Which mechanism dominates in a given case is an empirical question this
document does not resolve in advance. The targeted baseline neighbor
typically resolves to terminal lock rather than reorganization, since it
did not choose or prepare for the role (Corollary 10.5b.11a.1's
stripped-reorganization logic applies, but the predator's resulting
reorganization is generally a lesser version of it than an aligned-knock
route would produce, since baseline containers hold no accumulated
momentum of their own to lend --- this asymmetry needs empirical
calibration, not asserted as a specific ratio here).

\textbf{ii. Accidental Capture (Cascading Vortex).}

Driven by declining algebraic connectivity (λ₂(L(t)) → 0, §8.8a.v)
during a wider cascading failure, a baseline container can be drawn into
a discharge window purely through proximity --- via its coupling weight
w\_ij(t) rather than through deliberate targeting (i, above) or
fluid-mass alignment (§10.5b.7). Two branches, both real and not
reducible to one another:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Toward a failing container. The baseline container is pulled toward a
  container resolving to terminal lock; per Corollary 10.5b.11a.1's
  logic it typically locks alongside it, though survival is possible
  depending on its own mass or resource relative to the pulling
  container's discharge magnitude, and on the phase of the cycle at
  which it was captured. This document does not commit to a closed-form
  survival probability, flagging it as a further empirically-open item
  alongside those in §14.2.
\item
  Toward a reorganizing container. The baseline container is pulled
  toward a container resolving to full reorganization instead; here it
  can passively benefit from proximity to a successful reset rather than
  being harmed by it, consistent with Γ\_redir(t) being high, by
  construction, in this branch.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{iii. Sacrificial Lifeboat.}

Distinct from (i) and (ii): two comparably-struggling containers,
neither predatory nor accidentally swept in, where the discharge
asymmetry favors one's survival through the other's own trajectory
redirection. Decompose per §16.8's Definition 16.7 into a
strength-explained component and a remainder:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Logic-based. The strength-explained component alone accounts for the
  asymmetry --- the comparatively weaker container at the time ends up
  absorbing the discharge, following directly from the same comparison
  already used elsewhere in this document (§10.5b.7's mass-weighting
  logic, here applied between peers rather than within one container's
  layers).
\item
  Intention-based. A genuine remainder exists beyond the strength
  comparison --- per §16.8's Hypothesis, attributable to the sacrificing
  container's own pre-event messaging history, findable where
  confirmable via §16.7's backward-facing procedure, not derivable from
  either container's state at the moment of collision alone.
\end{itemize}

\emph{\textbf{A second, distinct discovery route for the intention-based
remainder.}} §16.7's messaging-history procedure is not the only way to
find the actual reason behind a specific instance of intention-based
sacrifice, and need not be treated as the default first resort. Where
the sacrificing container is itself recursively composed (§5's
child-container structure), an internal audit of its own child systems
--- their individual states, couplings, and resource allocations at the
moment of the sacrifice --- can surface the actual reason directly,
rather than inferring it backward from external messaging history alone:
a specific child system's own condition may be what the parent container
was actually protecting, in a way no record of the parent's own external
messaging would show. Both routes are legitimate and can be used
together; neither supersedes the other, and where they disagree, the
more direct evidentiary source (a child system's own confirmed state at
the time) should be weighted over the inferential one (reconstructed
messaging history), consistent with this document's general preference
for direct attestation over inference wherever both are available
(§10.5b.18.4a).

A third, distinct decision channel, computable in advance rather than
requiring after-the-fact discovery: a value-weighted asymmetry, where
the sacrifice is explained neither by the sacrificing container's own
comparative weakness (the logic-based channel) nor by an opaque
intention requiring investigation (the intention-based channel), but by
a direct, explicit comparison of value between the two containers -\/-
the healthy system voluntarily absorbing discharge because the container
more likely to fail is nevertheless the more valuable of the two to
preserve. Define V(C,t) ≥ 0, a value function supplied by the domain
adapter or model (analogous to other constitutive mappings this document
leaves to domain-specific specification -\/- reproductive value,
continuation of a shared genetic or resource lineage, or whatever the
domain's own notion of systemic worth actually is), and the value-based
sacrifice condition: a healthy container i voluntarily absorbs a
neighbor j's discharge, or transfers resources directly into j's own
continued viability at direct cost to itself, when V(j,t) \textgreater{}
V(i,t) and j's own collapse risk without that transfer is genuinely
elevated -\/- both conditions checkable in advance from each container's
own current state, unlike the intention-based channels necessarily
retrospective discovery. A biological instance grounds this concretely:
a male whose own reproductive contribution is already complete,
transferring his own body's resources directly into a mate's or
offspring's continued viability at the cost of his own survival, where
the transfer measurably reduces the receiving container's own real
collapse risk (a low-efficiency waste of the transferred resource would
be extraction or predation, not this channel; §10.5b.19.7's
metabolic-efficiency test still applies to distinguish them). This
channel is independent of, and can co-occur with, the two channels
already established above: a given instance of sacrifice may be
explained by strength, by intention, by value, or by some combination,
and the three are not mutually exclusive alternative explanations for
the same event so much as three distinct, separately-checkable factors
that may each contribute.

A further, specific version of the intention-based case: a container
facing terminal lock either way may choose which neighbor absorbs its
discharge, or choose to absorb a neighbor's discharge itself,
specifically to keep a wider cascade (§8.8a.v) from propagating further
--- self-sacrifice undertaken to limit collateral damage rather than for
reasons reducible to the strength comparison alone. This is a specific
instance of the intention-based remainder, not a distinct mechanism, and
is subject to the same §16.8 confirmation standard: it should be
reported as a hypothesis unless the sacrificing container's pre-event
record independently supports it.

A fourth, distinct decision channel, defined specifically for the
predator-prey case rather than for two comparably-struggling peers:
convergence-triggered redirection. A high-entropy container i, itself
approaching or past Flip Condition A's terminal-lock threshold
(M̃\_tip(i) ≥ M\_lock, §10.5b.9), coupled to a specific extraction target
j (§8.6a) it has been extracting from and displacing entropy onto, may
redirect its own discharge away from j specifically as a consequence of
its own metamessenger population's timing, rather than through a
strength comparison, a retrospectively-discovered intention, or an
explicit value judgment. Define t\_conv(i), the convergence onset time,
as the first time ω\_meta\^{}up(i,t) (Definition 10.5b.18.2) crosses a
fitted convergence threshold θ\_conv from below --- the point at which
i's own metamessenger population begins genuinely transmitting
operational reality to official, per the ordinary disclosure-pacing
account already given in §10.5b.18. Where t\_conv(i) occurs at or after
i's own terminal-lock time t\_lock(i) (§10.5b.9) --- convergence
arriving too late for i's own official layer to act on it in time to
avert i's own collapse --- but strictly before i's discharge event
completes, i's redirection channel is redirected away from j
specifically:

Γ\_redir(i→j, t) → 0 for t ≥ t\_conv(i), held for the remainder of the
discharge window, distinct from and running in the opposite direction to
Predatory Lifeboat's own Γ\_redir(i→k,t) (Definition 10.5b.11b.4), which
requires no convergence event of any kind.

This is a genuine fourth channel, not a redescription of the three
above: it is neither a strength comparison (i may be the stronger
container at the moment of discharge), nor an opaque intention requiring
§16.7's retrospective discovery (t\_conv(i) is directly computable from
a quantity, ω\_meta\^{}up(i,t), this document already tracks going
forward), nor a value comparison (no V(C,t) judgment is required, and no
domain-supplied worth mapping is needed for this channel to apply).
Falsifiable prediction: instances of this channel should show
ω\_meta\^{}up(i,t) crossing θ\_conv within a short, fitted window
immediately preceding i's own discharge event, distinguishable from
ordinary Sacrificial Lifeboat instances by this specific temporal
signature. This is also a strictly narrower window than convergence
arriving early enough to avert i's own collapse altogether, which this
document's existing anchor and external-feedback machinery (§10.5b.13,
§10.5b.18.9) already covers; this channel applies only to the case where
convergence is too late to save i but not too late to redirect what i's
own collapse does to j.

\textbf{iv. Predatory Lifeboat.}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11b.4 (Predatory Lifeboat).}} A container
i approaching Flip Condition A's terminal-lock threshold, with no
external anchor available, may launch a frantic strike (§8.8a) targeted
specifically at a low-entropy or otherwise healthy neighbor k, using k
as an involuntary redirection target:

M\_tip(i,t) recomputed against k's absorbed momentum, per §10.5b.11a's
redirection mechanics, but with the redirection channel Γ\_redir(t)
supplied coercively by i's own extraction (§8.6a) rather than
voluntarily by an anchor. This lets i reach the same non-terminal,
stripped-reorganization band §10.5b.11a describes, at k's direct
expense: k, lacking i's accumulated coherence advantage and not a
willing anchor, absorbs the discharge as a full terminal lock of its own
rather than a survivable redirection --- i tumbles into stripped
reorganization while k freezes. This is a real, distinct failure mode
external anchor-eligibility screening (§10.5b.13) exists specifically to
prevent: an eligible anchor's own designed capacity and voluntary
participation are what turn redirection into rescue rather than
sacrifice: an unscreened neighbor pressed into this role has neither.

\emph{Domain instances.} A failing firm transferring its collapse onto a
healthy partner through fraudulent conveyance; a failing bank pulling
down a sound counterparty.

\textbf{v. Cascading Vortex.}

Definition 10.5b.11b.5 (Cascading Vortex). A baseline container m, not
coupled to the collapsing container i and not a target of any extraction
or redirection, can still be drawn into i's tipping event purely through
network proximity: i's discharge/venting flux (H\_i, §8.6a --- renamed
here from ``unvented heat,'' which is now ℐ\_i's own role, not H\_i's,
since §8.8a.1 established H\_i as the instantaneous venting rate rather
than accumulated pressure) discharges multi-directionally as it
approaches terminal lock, and any container within m's own coupling
range (Λ\_im(t) \textgreater{} 0, even weakly) absorbs a real momentum
contribution proportional to that proximity --- this is a live-discharge
mechanism, driven by i's current venting flux as it actively discharges,
not by accumulated trapped pressure, and a real, specific route into
this mechanism now exists beyond ordinary approach-to-terminal-lock: the
Resolution-Kick-driven frantic striking described above (§8.8a)
discharges at whatever coupled neighbors N\_i(t) currently exist,
without regard to whether they had any involvement in the original
extraction --- a predator destabilized by a decoupling target's own
accurate kick can, in striking out to soothe that destabilization, draw
a previously-uninvolved baseline neighbor into the vortex the same way
sustained approach to terminal lock would. The grid-level consequence is
a real, indirect chain worth naming explicitly: a single parasitized
target's own successful resolution can propagate instability well beyond
its own predator, into containers that never had any coupling to either
the target or the original extraction at all, entirely through the
predator's own destabilized lashing-out rather than through any further
extraction on the predators part.

M\_tip(m,t) ← M\_tip(m,t) + κ\_vortex·Λ\_im(t)·H\_i(t), κ\_vortex
\textgreater{} 0 fitted --- an additive update, not set inclusion, which
was never a meaningful operator for a scalar momentum quantity and is
corrected here,

added to m's own existing tipping-momentum terms. No targeting
mechanism, predatory or otherwise, need be active for this term to be
nonzero --- it is a structural consequence of coupling proximity to an
active discharge event, not a choice by i, m, or anything between them.
This supplies the previously missing forcing pathway by which the
cascade can reach otherwise-uninvolved baseline containers, rather than
remaining confined to i's already-coupled predatory or sacrificial
neighbors; whether the induced momentum actually drives m toward
tipping, and whether enough such inductions actually produce λ₂(L(t)) →
0 (Definition 8.8a.4) at the network level, is determined by m's own
downstream tipping dynamics and the networks aggregate response, not
established by this term alone.

\emph{\textbf{Refinement to purpose-built anchors (§10.5b.13's
designed-capacity treatment).}} An anchor's safety was established there
as a static design property --- eligible up to its designed capacity, an
ordinary coupled neighbor beyond it. A real, additional dynamic behavior
belongs alongside that static property, not replacing it: an eligible
purpose-built anchor does not merely have sufficient capacity, it
actively tracks the anchored container's own trajectory while absorbing
discharge, and its own parameters (a\_i, e\_i) reset toward baseline
once the discharge is fully absorbed, rather than accumulating
unresolved strain the way an ordinary coupled neighbor's would. This is
what distinguishes a genuinely engineered shield from a merely
high-capacity neighbor that happens not to have failed yet.

\textbf{vi. Structural Self-Shielding (Reverse Predatory Lifeboat).}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11b.6 (Structural Self-Shielding).}} A
high-entropy container i whose own collapse is driven by isolation
rather than extraction --- every previously active coupling having
independently decoupled, Λ\_ij(t) → 0 for each neighbor j i was not
itself extracting from, per §8.5's own coupling dynamics, rather than by
any deliberate withdrawal on i's part --- undergoes a rise in its own
feedback permeability as those couplings close, Π\_f(t) approaching its
ceiling by the same self-sourced mechanics already given for Voluntary
Flood (Definition 10.5b.18.16), here driven by isolation rather than by
i's own deliberate choice to flood. This rising permeability brings i's
own comfort-aligned population into forced alignment with official ---
ω\_meta\^{}up(i,t) rising toward 1 by the same disclosure mechanics as
Definition 10.5b.18.2 --- not through the paced, deliberate release that
definition ordinarily describes, but as a consequence of the firewall's
own supporting structure (the coupled neighbors whose extraction
previously funded the firewall's own maintenance cost, §10.5b.18.4)
having withdrawn out from under it.

Where this alignment completes while i retains nonzero tipping momentum
M\_tip(i,t) and a genuinely vulnerable neighbor k exists within i's
remaining coupling range (Λ\_ik(t) \textgreater{} 0, k itself
approaching its own terminal-lock band), i's discharge may be
voluntarily redirected to absorb k's own momentum rather than to
displace i's own momentum onto k:

M\_tip(k,t) ← M\_tip(k,t) − κ\_shield·Λ\_ik(t)·min(M\_tip(i,t),
M\_tip(k,t)), κ\_shield \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

with i's own discharge computed against the absorbed quantity per
§10.5b.11a's redirection mechanics --- the exact mechanical inverse of
Predatory Lifeboat (Definition 10.5b.11b.4), which instead adds a
coercively-redirected quantity onto an unwilling neighbor's own M\_tip.
Structural Self-Shielding requires the same redirection machinery run in
the opposite direction and the opposite valence: i absorbs on k's
behalf, at i's own full cost, rather than i offloading onto k at k's
expense. i's own resolution under this channel is not itself improved by
the shielding act --- i does not thereby gain a survivable, non-terminal
outcome the way Predatory Lifeboat's predator does --- and i's own
terminal-lock probability for its own discharge window is unaffected by
κ\_shield or by the transfer itself; what changes is k's outcome, not
i's. This is the formal content of a negentropic act performed by a
container in the course of its own, unaverted collapse: the collapse
itself is not prevented or mitigated for i, but i's own momentum is
spent shielding a neighbor rather than extracting from one, in i's final
active window.

This is distinct from the value-weighted Sacrificial-Lifeboat channel
(§10.5b.11b.iii, third channel) in both trigger and population:
value-weighted sacrifice requires an explicit V(C,t) comparison and
applies between two comparably-struggling peer containers, computable in
advance from each container's current state; Structural Self-Shielding
requires no value comparison at all, applies specifically to an
isolation-collapsing high-entropy container acting on behalf of a
separate, not-necessarily-peer neighbor, and is triggered mechanically
by the specific sequence of decoupling → rising Π\_f(t) → forced
ω\_meta\^{}up convergence, rather than by any advance judgment of
relative worth. The two channels are not mutually exclusive --- a given
instance could in principle satisfy both --- but neither is a
restatement of the other.

Falsifiable prediction. Instances of Structural Self-Shielding should
show, in sequence: a measurable decline in i's own active coupling count
preceding i's own terminal decline (isolation, not extraction, as the
proximate driver); a corresponding rise in ω\_meta\^{}up(i,t) coincident
with, not preceding, that isolation (ruling out ordinary paced
disclosure, which this document already associates with a sustained,
gradual trend rather than an isolation-coincident one); and a measurable
reduction in a specific neighbor k's own M\_tip(t) trajectory, timed to
i's own discharge window, without any corresponding improvement in i's
own terminal-lock probability. The absence of that third signature --- a
shielded neighbor's improved outcome unaccompanied by any improvement in
i's own --- is what distinguishes this channel from an ordinary
mutual-benefit coupling, and its opposite sign is what distinguishes it
from Predatory Lifeboat.

\hypertarget{b.11c-active-search-search-suppression-and-mirror-threat-avoidance}{%
\subsection{10.5b.11c Active Search, Search Suppression, and
Mirror-Threat
Avoidance}\label{b.11c-active-search-search-suppression-and-mirror-threat-avoidance}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.1 (Active Search Intensity).}}

For a high-entropy container i, define Σ\_search(i,t) ≥ 0, the rate at
which i initiates evaluation of candidate coupling targets beyond its
existing edges --- distinct from ρ(j), which governs persistence of
couplings already formed. Where i satisfies §10.5b.18.25's structural
extraction dependence, Σ\_search(i,t) scales with i's own deficit:

Σ\_search(i,t) = κ\_search · max(0, M\_maint(C,t) + M\_decep(t) −
P\_prod(t)), κ\_search \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

so that search intensity tracks the severity of i's own unmet need,
rather than being a free-floating behavioral parameter. A container not
satisfying structural extraction dependence has Σ\_search(i,t) = 0 by
this default, though an adapter may supply a nonzero value directly
where a domain has independent evidence of active-seeking behavior
absent formal dependence.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.1a (Target Selection Among
Candidates).}} Σ\_search(i,t) governs how intensely i evaluates
candidates beyond its existing edges; it does not by itself determine
which candidate is actually selected where several are being evaluated
at once. Selection among candidates \{C\_k\} is jointly determined by
proximity and target-type compatibility, reusing machinery already built
rather than introducing new primitives:

Select(i,t) = argmax\_k {[} w\_prox · Prox(i,k,t) + w\_type ·
𝒱\_\{i→k\}(t) {]}, w\_prox + w\_type = 1, both fitted,

where Prox(i,k,t) is i's positional proximity to candidate k (derived
from existing network distance or positional embedding, §8.6a.7, where
available) and 𝒱\_\{i→k\}(t) (§8.6a.4) is i's own observation-mapped
estimate of k's vulnerability, not k's true latent state. This is
distinct from Definition 8.3.1's first-contact attraction, which governs
general network re-entry toward cohesive-looking candidates for any
newly-uncoupled container; this definition governs specifically which
extraction candidate a high-entropy container under active search
selects, driven by exploitability rather than cohesion. During a frantic
striking phase (§8.8a.2) specifically, w\_prox rises relative to its
ordinary fitted value --- accelerating divergence narrows deliberation
and favors whichever compatible candidate is nearest, consistent with
this document's own account of frantic striking as thermodynamic
discharge rather than strategic search.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.2 (Search Suppression by Ambient
Cohesion).}}

Active search does not operate at full strength against an arbitrary
population; its effective reach is degraded by the ambient cohesion
density of the containers it searches among. Define

Σ\_search\^{}eff(i,t) = Σ\_search(i,t) · exp{[}−κ\_amb · Λ\_amb(t){]},

where Λ\_amb(t) is the resource-weighted mean of §9.10.1's
Λ\_int(C\_k,t) across all candidates \{C\_k\} within i's observable
search range, and κ\_amb \textgreater{} 0 is fitted. A search population
that is, on average, densely coupled among itself suppresses i's
effective search reach exponentially rather than linearly --- consistent
with the document's own imagery for this mechanism, in which a
population's mutual coupling does not merely fail to attract a predator
(§8.3.1's Corollary 8.3.1.1 already covers that passive case) but
actively degrades an already-searching predator's ability to locate a
target at all.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11c.1 (Grid-Level Predator Starvation,
Search Channel).}}

Where Λ\_amb(t) rises across a network --- driven by the same
cohesion-building dynamics §9.10.1 and §8.3.1 already establish ---
every structurally extraction-dependent container's
Σ\_search\^{}eff(i,t) falls in tandem, independent of and additional to
§8.3.1's passive-attraction channel. The two channels close different
routes to the same target: Corollary 8.3.1.1 closes the route by which
new entrants are drawn toward a predator; this proposition closes the
route by which a predator's own search finds them. Both failing
simultaneously is what gives grid-wide predator starvation its full
force as new entrants avoid isolated predators by default while isolated
predators simultaneously lose the ability to find anyone at all.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.3 (Mirror-Threat Suppression).}}

Independent of resource-based extraction incentive, define a suppression
factor on coupling likelihood between two high-entropy containers i and
j:

ζ\_mirror(i,j,t) = exp{[}−κ\_mirror · min(D\_op,i(t), D\_op,j(t)){]},
κ\_mirror \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

applied multiplicatively to whichever edge-formation quantity (Ξ, §8.3;
A\_first, §8.3.1; or a search-initiated contact under Definition
10.5b.11c.1) would otherwise govern the i-j pairing. Both parties must
register as entropic (both D\_op values contributing via the minimum)
for this suppression to activate meaningfully; a single entropic party
paired with a non-entropic one does not trigger it.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11c.2 (Mirror-Threat Is Additive to, Not
Subsumed by, Resource Suppression).}}

§8.6a's X\_\{i,j\} already scales with target resources R\_j, so
extraction incentive between two severely resource-depleted high-entropy
containers is independently low without any mirror-threat mechanism.
Definition 10.5b.11c.3 does additional work precisely where resource
depletion does not already suppress coupling: two high-entropy
containers of differing severity, where the less-depleted party would
otherwise present exploitable resources to the more-depleted,
more-motivated party (§10.5b.18.25's dependent container searching under
Definition 10.5b.11c.1). Pure resource logic predicts this pairing
should occur at a nontrivial rate; ζ\_mirror(i,j,t) predicts it should
occur markedly less often than resource logic alone implies, because
recognizing another container's own entropic signature functions as a
threat signal --- the prospective target's own extractive capacity, not
only its resource level, is what a high-entropy searcher is registering
and avoiding.

\textbf{10.5b.12 Combined Intervention}

For both regimes, anchoring (a mechanical/behavioral intervention,
arresting oscillation and extraction) and active messaging change (an
informational intervention --- external correction, or the internal
rogue-metamessenger pathway of §10.5b.14) are more effective together
than either alone, whether applied pre-flip as prevention or post-flip
as recovery: anchoring alone can halt an oscillation without resolving
what caused it; messaging change alone, absent containment, risks
triggering F\_conf's destabilizing surge before it can take effect.
Formalized directly, rather than left as narrative alone: define each
intervention's own success probability independently, Anchor\_succ(t)
(halting oscillation, §10.5b.13) and Message\_succ(t) (correcting the
underlying divergence without triggering F\_conf, §10.5b.14). Applied
together rather than separately, real synergy exists specifically
because each intervention removes the condition that makes the other
risky:

Combined\_succ(t) = Anchor\_succ(t) + Message\_succ(t) −
Anchor\_succ(t)·Message\_succ(t) +
κ\_synergy·Anchor\_succ(t)·Message\_succ(t)·(1 −
Anchor\_succ(t)·Message\_succ(t)), κ\_synergy \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where the first three terms are the ordinary probability of at least one
succeeding if independent, and the κ\_synergy term is the real,
additional gain specific to combination: anchoring removes messaging
change's own main risk (an uncontained F\_conf surge, since oscillation
is already arrested), and messaging change removes anchoring's own main
limitation (resolving root cause rather than only symptoms), so each
intervention's individual success probability is itself higher in
combination than its solo value used above --- κ\_synergy \textgreater{}
0 is the formal claim that this is genuine synergy, not merely two
independent chances stacked, and is directly falsifiable: observed
combined success rates exceeding the independence-only baseline (the
first three terms alone) support κ\_synergy \textgreater{} 0; rates
matching that baseline would falsify the synergy claim specifically,
without touching either intervention's own individual effectiveness.

\textbf{10.5b.12b Counter-Momentum Availability}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.12b.1 (Counter-Momentum Availability).}}

For container i approaching a Flip Condition B event at time t, define:

\emph{Λ}\_counter(i,t) = Σ\_j Λ\_ij(t) · max(0,
−\textbf{g}\_C(i,t)·û\_ji(t)),

reusing the network coupling strength Λ\_ij (§8.5) and the structural
grain vector g\_C(t) already defined in §4.11.1, summed over
currently-coupled neighbors j whose own recent coupling direction toward
i opposes i's own dominant recent oscillation direction. This measures
the real, currently-available momentum-absorbing capacity in the network
immediately around i, not a hypothetical or historical one --- a
neighbor that has since decoupled or reversed its own orientation
contributes nothing.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.12b.1 (Backstop-Dependent Resolution).}}

Holding mass-weighting (μ\_o, μ\_p) fixed, a Flip Condition B event
resolves differently depending on Λ\_counter(i,t): where Λ\_counter(i,t)
exceeds a fitted threshold Λ\_backstop, the incoming discharge is
absorbed elastically against the positioned neighbor and the container
self-arrests (§6.9's bounded oscillation) rather than completing a
tipping outcome at all; where Λ\_counter(i,t) falls below that
threshold, the same discharge instead completes as a full 360°
reorganization (§10.5b.9's B2), since no real structure exists to return
the momentum to. This is a genuinely separate mechanism from
mass-weighting, not a restatement of it: a container can carry
mass-weighting favoring B2 and still self-arrest if a real backstop is
present, or carry mass-weighting favoring self-arrest and still complete
a 360° reorganization if no backstop is available at the moment of
tipping. Where an adapter cannot supply real, current coupling-direction
data for neighbors, Λ\_counter(i,t) defaults to 0 (no assumed backstop),
which is the conservative default consistent with this document's
general preference for not silently assuming protective structure that
has not been confirmed.

\textbf{10.5b.13 Anchor Eligibility}

A low-entropy baseline container can safely anchor a destabilizing
neighbor, but only by actively maintaining zero extraction and zero
displacement flux for the full duration of the intervention --- i.e.,
holding X\_\{a,i\}(t) = 0 (§8.6a) throughout, a maintained behavioral
condition, not a structural guarantee. Lapsing on this vigilance risks
the anchor itself being drawn into an extraction cycle and reclassified
into the medium-entropy regime --- becoming a casualty rather than a
stabilizer.

A semi-rigid horizontal field --- a boundary configuration with no
exploitable structure to extract from or displace onto, per §2.3 --- is
structurally, not behaviorally, safe: X = 0 by construction, not by
maintained effort. This is the preferred anchor wherever available, but
is characteristically rare, particularly in human systems.

A third, structurally distinct type: a container or sub-structure
purpose-built or purpose-designated specifically to absorb another's
discharge --- a dedicated buffering structure maintained for this role,
rather than a general-purpose neighbor pressed into service. Unlike the
vigilant baseline container (whose safety is a maintained behavioral
condition) and the semi-rigid horizontal field (whose safety is
structural, requiring no maintained effort), a purpose-built anchor's
safety is a design property: it is safe to the extent it was actually
engineered for the load it absorbs, and its eligibility should be
assessed against its adapter-supplied designed capacity rather than
assumed. Exceeding that capacity converts it from an anchor into an
ordinary coupled neighbor, subject to the same risks as any other.

\emph{\textbf{Two distinct anchor-failure modes.}} Anchor eligibility as
established above answers whether an anchor is competent to absorb a
given discharge; it does not distinguish two genuinely different ways an
anchor can fail the container it is anchoring, which require different
correction:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Accidental wrong feedback.} An eligible, genuinely
  well-intentioned anchor whose own signal to the anchored container is
  simply miscalibrated --- wrong about what the container actually
  needs, without any exploitative intent. Correction is informational:
  the anchor's own feedback loop needs better data, not removal from the
  anchoring role.
\item
  \textbf{Deliberate predation.} An anchor whose signal is calibrated
  correctly for the anchor's own benefit rather than the anchored
  container's, exploiting the anchoring relationship's trust rather than
  failing to understand it. Correction requires removing the anchor from
  the role, since the failure is not informational.
\end{itemize}

Both are real, destructive failure modes producing similarly-damaged
outcomes for the anchored container, which is exactly why they need to
be told apart rather than merged into one ``bad anchor'' category:
applying the wrong correction (retraining a deliberately predatory
anchor, or removing an accidentally miscalibrated one who could have
been corrected) wastes the intervention.

\emph{\textbf{The dangerous-anchor typology.}} A further, real category
exists beyond both: an anchor that is genuinely, measurably effective at
anchoring some targets while being genuinely dangerous to others, where
the danger is not a failure of competence at all but a direct
consequence of the anchor's own underlying motivation. Define
Motive(a,t) as the adapter-supplied or inferred degree to which anchor
a's own participation is self-interested (deriving cover, power, or
standing from the anchoring role itself, rather than from the anchored
container's outcome):

Danger(a,j,t) = f(Motive(a,t), Vulnerability(j,t)), increasing in both,

where an anchor with high measured competence and high Motive(a,t) is
correctly eligible by the competence criterion above and simultaneously
dangerous to targets whose own vulnerability profile happens to serve
that motivation --- competence and danger are not opposites here, and
screening on competence alone, without also screening Motive(a,t),
systematically misses this category.

\hypertarget{b.13.1-mutual-anchor-clusters}{%
\subsection{10.5b.13.1 Mutual Anchor
Clusters}\label{b.13.1-mutual-anchor-clusters}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13.1 (Mutual Anchor Cluster).}}

A set of containers \{C\_1,\ldots,C\_n\}, each in the low-entropy
baseline or medium-entropy regime, forms a mutual anchor cluster at time
t when their pairwise internal cohesion (Λ\_int over the set, §9.10.1)
exceeds a fitted threshold Λ\_mutual, and no individual member satisfies
any of §10.5b.13's three existing anchor-eligibility criteria on its
own. This is the defining structural difference from an ordinary anchor:
eligibility here is a property of the cluster's own coupling density,
not of any single member's behavioral vigilance, structural safety, or
designed capacity.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13.1a (Reciprocal Support Density).}}

Within a mutual anchor cluster, §10.5b.18.9--18.11's external feedback
channel φ\_fb(i,j,t) and support flux Γ\_supp(i,j,t) --- defined
pairwise for an ordinary healthy-neighbor coupling --- are computed for
every ordered pair (i,j) within the cluster rather than in one
privileged direction. Define the cluster's reciprocal support density,

Φ\_mutual(t) = {[}Σ\_\{i,j ∈ cluster, i≠j\} Λ\_ij(t)·φ\_fb(i,j,t){]} /
{[}n(n−1){]},

the cluster-averaged bidirectional feedback rate, and analogously
Γ\_mutual(t) for support flux. Unlike a single anchor's one-directional
supply, every member of a mutual anchor cluster is simultaneously a
source and a recipient of both quantities --- Definition 10.5b.18.11's
correction forcing terms F\_ext,fb and F\_ext,supp apply to every
member's own D\_om(t) using the cluster's aggregate Φ\_mutual(t) and
Γ\_mutual(t) as their driving input, rather than each member needing a
distinct external anchor of its own.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13.1 (Distributed Margin Benefit).}}

Each member's hull-breach margin (§16.3's margin\_i(t)) gains a positive
contribution from cluster membership,

margin\_i\^{}cluster(t) = margin\_i(t) + κ\_cluster ·
Φ\_mutual(t)·Γ\_mutual(t), κ\_cluster \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

distributing protective effect across the cluster rather than
concentrating it in one designated anchor's continued vigilance. This
has a direct consequence for cluster resilience under §8.8a's cascading
dynamics: where an ordinary anchor's failure or withdrawal removes
protection entirely (a single point of failure, consistent with
§10.5b.13's warning that a lapsing vigilant anchor becomes a casualty
itself), a mutual anchor cluster's protective effect degrades gradually
as Φ\_mutual(t) and Γ\_mutual(t) decline with membership loss, rather
than failing discontinuously when any single member is lost. A cluster
of size n loses protective capacity roughly in proportion to the
coupling density removed with a departing member, not catastrophically
as an ordinary anchor's failure would.

\hypertarget{b.13.2-permeability-flooding-in-high-entropy-containers-and-the-anchors-containment-role}{%
\subsection{10.5b.13.2 Permeability Flooding in High-Entropy Containers,
and the Anchor's Containment
Role}\label{b.13.2-permeability-flooding-in-high-entropy-containers-and-the-anchors-containment-role}}

Definition 10.5b.13.2 (Real Causes of Permeability Flooding). Π\_f(i,t)
can rise past the firewall's own maintained resistance through several
real, distinct pathways, none of which involve an anchor: (a)
Extraction/displacement threshold --- a sufficiently large cumulative
volume of extraction and displacement (Σ\_j X\_\{i,j\}, H\_i, §8.6a), or
a small number of particularly egregious acts, rather than a single
fixed trigger event. (b) Parasite-to-predator escalation --- a shift in
the container's own extraction character from sustained, sustainable
parasitism to fast, destructive predation (§8.6a.6's degrees of
parasitism), itself driving (c) below faster than steady-state
extraction would. (c) Resource-exhaustion of the suppression apparatus,
the shared mechanism underlying (a) and (b): M\_decep's own real cost
(§10.5b.18.4) rises with extraction severity and divergence, and unpaid
M\_decep debt accrues into δR (§7.9) exactly as any other resource debt
does. Once that debt exceeds what the container can continue paying, the
firewall (§10.5b.18.1) fails not because anything forced it, but because
the container can no longer afford to keep maintaining it --- chaos
previously confined to operational begins to permeate official's own
understanding and sense of self as well, not only its operational layer,
a natural consequence of exhaustion rather than an imposed event. (d)
Decoupling-triggered external feedback --- not a separately-invented
phenomenon, but the specific, already-formalized case of a Resolution
Kick (§8.8b) landing on the extracting container itself. Where a
medium-entropy target j completes its own tipping event while still
coupled to its extractor i, K\_j(t) reaches i carrying real, accurate
signal about the extraction j actually experienced (§8.8b.1) --- a
violent transfer of accurate information. Whether this kick produces
flooding or is successfully absorbed is a real race condition: define
the patching rate Ṗ\_patch(i,t) as the rate at which i's
operational-aligned metamessengers can rewrite or resuppress incoming
accurate signal via M\_o,t\^{}false (§10.5b.14.1), and the kick's own
incoming rate as K\_j(t)/τ\_kick for some real absorption window
τ\_kick. Where Ṗ\_patch(i,t) ≥ K\_j(t)/τ\_kick, the container
successfully re-suppresses the accurate signal before it registers, and
Π\_f(i,t) does not rise from this event; where Ṗ\_patch(i,t) \textless{}
K\_j(t)/τ\_kick, patching cannot keep pace, and the excess feeds
directly into (c)'s resource-exhaustion mechanism, accelerating the
container toward flooding rather than causing it outright on its own.
(e) Voluntary self-relinquishment --- a fifth cause, distinct from all
four above in being the container's own deliberate act rather than an
external or resource-driven event: a high-entropy container that
genuinely admits its own operational reality to itself and relinquishes
its own control-shaped suppression of that reality does not thereby
generate new divergence (§10.5b.19.8's mechanism does not apply here),
but releases divergence that was already present and already accruing
cost, simply held back by the very suppression effort now being
relinquished. This release is, for a high-entropy container
specifically, itself flooding: the operational reality that M\_decep and
M\_o,t\^{}false had been working to keep from official's own awareness
becomes visible all at once, precisely because the suppression mechanism
producing that concealment is what has just been switched off. This is
not a failure mode alongside (a)-(d); it is the single legitimate route
into flooding compatible with the Do No Harm Principle's medium-entropy
exception (§10.5b.14.3b) extended to the high-entropy case: real health
requires the concealment to end, and ending it is flooding, by
construction, for a system whose entire official/operational split was
built on that concealment continuing. The container facing this route
requires the same sustained external anchoring (§10.5b.10) any other
flooding event requires, precisely because self-relinquishment does not
reduce the crisis, it exposes one already fully accrued.

None of (a) through (d) requires or involves an external anchor. An
anchor's real function, defined here for the first time, is containment
of a flooding event these mechanisms have already produced, not
production of the flood itself: Contain(i,t) = 1 while an eligible
anchor (§10.5b.13) actively holds i stable --- absorbing discharge,
restricting extraction and displacement --- for the duration of the
instability (a)--(d) already caused, without itself contributing to
Π\_f(i,t)'s own rise.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13.2.1 (Operational Death and Forced
Percolation).}} Once Π\_f\^{}forced(i,t) exceeds the firewall's
resistance for a sustained duration, the corrupted operational structure
the firewall was maintaining can no longer be sustained against the
now-unblocked feedback, and undergoes a genuine structural collapse
distinct from ordinary gradual correction --- the previous operational
organization ceases functioning before any replacement structure has
taken over, a real transitional state of zero organized structure, not a
smooth handoff. This transitional collapse necessarily produces a real,
elevated period of structural fragmentation and oscillation
(s\_frag(i,t) and σ\_p(i,t) both spike sharply, §10.5b.1), consistent
with and predicted directly by this mechanism rather than an incidental
side effect to be minimized. Only once this transitional collapse has
occurred does the container's own official-layer content --- established
at §10.5b.2.13 as genuinely accurate and intact throughout, merely cut
off from updating --- gain the open channel needed to flow downward into
the now-unoccupied operational layer, replacing the destroyed corrupted
structure with genuine negentropic organization rather than suppressing
the old structure's behavior while leaving it intact underneath.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13.2.2 (Strength and Duration
Requirements).}} This mechanism requires anchoring substantially
stronger and longer than the medium-entropy case (§10.5b.13's ordinary
anchor eligibility), for three compounding, real reasons rather than
one: the anchor must first supply enough sustained A\_flood(t) to
overcome active firewall resistance, not merely pre-existing passive
inertia; it must remain stable and continue supplying that flooding
through the transitional collapse itself, precisely when the anchored
container is at its most volatile and most likely to discharge
instability onto anything nearby (§8.8a's cascading dynamics); and it
must sustain long enough for genuine percolation to complete, not merely
long enough for surface oscillation to subside, since the latter can
occur well before the former and would read externally as success while
the underlying corrupted structure remains intact. An anchor withdrawn
after oscillation subsides but before percolation completes should
expect relapse into the original corrupted configuration, since the
firewall's own resistance --- reduced but not structurally replaced ---
reasserts itself once the forcing lifts.

10.5b.13a Anchoring as Categorically Non-Reciprocal, and the
Grid-Saturation Forced Choice

Proposition 10.5b.13a.1 (Anchoring Is Not a Coupling Relationship). The
X\_\{a,i\}(t) = 0 condition already established above (§10.5b.13)
constrains only the anchor's own outbound flux toward the anchored
container. Stated explicitly here for the first time: the full anchoring
relationship is strictly one-directional feedback, anchor → system only,
with extraction and displacement disallowed in *both* directions for the
relationship to remain anchoring at all -\/- X\_\{i,a\}(t) = 0 as well
as X\_\{a,i\}(t) = 0. This is not an instance of ordinary network
coupling Λ\_ij (§8.5) with one direction happening to sit at zero; it is
a categorically different kind of connection, since Λ\_ij presumes
genuine bidirectional exchange capacity that anchoring specifically
excludes. This is precisely what makes anchoring safe in a way ordinary
coupling with a struggling neighbor is not: the anchor carries no
reciprocal exposure to extraction, by definition of remaining an anchor,
not merely by favorable circumstance.

Definition 10.5b.13a.2 (Calculated-Risk Protector-Coupling, Contrasted).
A structurally distinct relationship exists alongside anchoring, easily
confused with it but mechanically opposite: a container voluntarily
entering genuine, reciprocal coupling with a high-entropy neighbor
specifically *because* extraction under that neighbor's protection is
assessed as preferable to likely destruction while unprotected in a
hostile grid. Unlike anchoring, this relationship involves real, ongoing
extraction (X\_\{i,j\}(t) \textgreater{} 0 in the extractive direction),
entered open-eyed as a calculated tradeoff rather than as a failure to
recognize predation. The choice is rational under the specific
constraint of the surrounding grid's hostility, even though the coupling
itself is genuinely extractive and would be correctly classified as
ordinary predation (§8.6a) absent that context.

\emph{Domain instances.} A sacrificial anode, which couples with a
corrosive environment to protect a ship's hull; firebreaks; a supervisor
deliberately embedding with a risky bank.

Proposition 10.5b.13a.3 (Grid-Saturation Forced Choice). Where a
container's local grid neighborhood is saturated with high-entropy
oscillation -\/- every neighbor within coupling range is either actively
knocking or itself being knocked, per §8.8a's dynamics -\/- and no
anchor meeting §10.5b.13's eligibility conditions exists anywhere within
that range, achieving stability without extraction is a genuine
structural impossibility, not merely a difficult outcome: any
locally-stable configuration achievable without an anchor requires the
container itself to supply the stabilizing extraction, which is
definitionally what makes a container high-entropy in the first place.
This forces an exhaustive partition over the container's available
options: (i) become high-entropy itself, extracting to buy local
stability; (ii) flee the saturated vicinity entirely, since remaining
within range while declining extraction is the excluded case; (iii)
enter calculated-risk protector-coupling per Definition 10.5b.13a.2,
accepting extraction as the price of protection; or (iv) locate and
anchor the actual source of the surrounding cascade directly -\/-
stabilizing the perturbation itself rather than the container's own
position relative to it, potentially halting the grid-wide oscillation
without requiring any of the first three options at all.

\emph{Scope.} This proposition's exclusion of remaining in range while
declining extraction holds at saturation specifically. Below saturation
the configuration is not excluded: it is boundary defense (§10.5b.13b),
the most robust strategy available at every grid intensity short of
saturation. Corollary 10.5b.13b.9 supplies the mechanism by which it
fails at saturation itself --- not through strikes, which hardening can
deflect, but through isolation, since open exchange is possible only
between open systems.

\textbf{10.5b.13c One-Directional Anchor Capacity}

The engine reads a container's internal distress: how close it is to its
tipping point and how fast it is sliding (§10.3a). Whether that distress
becomes collapse depends on how much external, one-directional support
will absorb the container's deficit, which is the anchor capacity A(t)
of Definition 10.3a.7. Break-even is the tipping point for every
container; anchors move the point of no return. This section measures
anchor capacity from flows that can be observed, under §10.5b.13's
eligibility conditions, and combines it with the engine's reading as an
additive correction, the one-directional analogue of Proposition
10.5b.13.1's distributed margin benefit. It is never merged into the
engine's own quantities.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13c.1 (Anchor Flow and Commitment
Share).}}

For each one-directional anchor type k, the anchor flow F\_k(t) ≥ 0 is
the resource the anchor supplies to the container per unit time without
a matching claim on the container's own exchange. Its commitment share
is the flow relative to the container's scale of operations, averaged
over the three periods ending at t so that it represents an established
commitment and not a single period's grant:

f\_k(t) = (1/3) · Σ\_\{s = t−2\}\^{}\{t\} F\_k(s) / W(s).

Instances: a government appropriation to a public college, hospital or
school district; donor gifts to a college, a congregation or a
nonprofit.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13c.2 (Structural Anchor: Parent
Membership).}}

S\_par(t) ∈ \{0, 1\} is 1 if the container belongs to a parent that can
absorb a member's deficit, and 0 otherwise. A parent that extracts from
its members instead of absorbing their losses is not an anchor
(§10.5b.13: an anchor holds extraction at zero), so membership in a
parent that draws a return from its members, such as a for-profit chain,
sets S\_par = 0.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13c.3 (Anchor Capacity).}}

A(t) = W(t) · {[} Σ\_k θ\_k · f\_k(t) + θ\_par · S\_par(t) {]}, θ\_k,
θ\_par ≥ 0,

with scale-free form Ã(t) = A(t) / W(t). Each θ\_k is a fitted
absorption multiple: how many units of deficit an anchor of type k will
absorb per unit of its established flow; θ\_par is the share of a
member's annual operations its parent will absorb. §10.5b.13 requires
that an anchor's eligibility be assessed against its actual capacity and
not assumed, which is why capacity is built from measured flows and not
from labels: an affiliation that brings no flow contributes nothing.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13c.4 (Anchored Margin).}}

Let M(t) be the engine's distress margin, the negative log-odds of a
distress classifier's probability, M(t) = −logit p(t), large when little
distress is read. The anchored margin is

M\_anch(t) = M(t) + κ\_anch · Ã(t), κ\_anch \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

the one-directional analogue of margin\_i\^{}cluster = margin\_i +
κ\_cluster · Φ\_mutual · Γ\_mutual (Proposition 10.5b.13.1). Because
κ\_anch and the θ enter only as products, they are fitted together as
non-negative coefficients κ\_anch · θ.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13c.1 (Anchoring can only clear a flag,
never raise one).}}

Since every κ\_anch · θ ≥ 0 and every f\_k, S\_par ≥ 0, M\_anch(t) ≥
M(t). At a common cut, every container flagged under the anchored margin
is also flagged under the engine's own margin; anchoring can only remove
flags. Any improvement from anchor capacity must therefore come from
clearing flags on containers that survive, the run-flat effect, and not
from catching collapses the engine missed. ∎

\textbf{Measurement rule.} Every anchor flow is read from records dated
at or before the time of evaluation. An anchor that arrives later is
part of the outcome being evaluated, not of anchor capacity at that
time; a later rescue enters as a new anchor (Proposition 10.3a.9).

\textbf{Inactive configuration.} With every θ = 0 (or no anchor flow
observed), A(t) = 0 and M\_anch = M: the point of no return reduces to
R\_avail − δR, and §10.3a is unchanged.

\textbf{10.5b.13b Counterpart-Conditional Boundaries, Hardening Latency,
and the Kinetic Plane}

§10.5b.13a establishes what a container can do once its grid is
saturated. This subsection supplies the account that applies everywhere
short of saturation: how a container remains safe among high-entropy
neighbors without becoming high-entropy itself, without paying for
protection with extraction, and without leaving. Its central claim is
that the healthiest containers are distinguished neither by
internalizing entropy (the high-entropy strategy, which buys short-term
stability at the cost of eventual collapse and grid-wide disturbance in
proportion to the container's own power) nor by externalizing it (which
produces the same over-leverage by another route), but by boundary
defense. Every quantity below is built from machinery already defined
--- the extraction flux (§8.6a), the tipping-momentum integral
(§10.5b.7), the resource balance (§7.4), threat perception and its
confidence gating (§10.5b.2.3--2.4), and target selection
(§10.5b.11c.1a) --- and each new term reduces to the prior formulation
when set to zero.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13b.1 (Counterpart-Conditional
Boundary).}} A container's realized boundary B\_t (Definition 2.3) is
not a single setting applied uniformly to its environment but a
specification conditional on what reaches it. For container j and
counterpart i, write B\_t(j; i), and represent it by a hardening state
β\_h(j,i,t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}, 0 fully open toward i and 1 fully hardened
against i. The boundary gates both admissible exchange and extraction:

E\_exch,ij\^{}adm(t) = (1 − β\_h(j,i,t)) · E\_exch,ij(t),
X\_\{i,j\}\^{}eff(t) = (1 − β\_h(j,i,t)) · X\_\{i,j\}(t),

where E\_exch,ij(t) is the exchange term of §7.4's resource balance
attributable to counterpart i, X\_\{i,j\}(t) is §8.6a's extraction flux,
and §7.4's balance is read with E\_exch = Σ\_i E\_exch,ij\^{}adm and
with extraction drawn down at X\_\{i,j\}\^{}eff. β\_h ≡ 0 recovers the
unbounded-exchange formulation exactly.

What reaches a boundary is classified by j as extractive to degree

c(j,i,t) = 1 / \{1 + exp{[}−κ\_c · (Effective\_perceived\_danger(j,i,t)
− θ\_protect(j,t)){]}\}, κ\_c \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

reusing Definition 10.5b.2.4's Effective\_perceived\_danger and the
θ\_protect threshold of §10.5b.2.20's signal-classification account, and
the hardening state evolves as

dβ\_h/dt = c(j,i,t) · (1 − β\_h(j,i,t)) / τ\_h(j,i,t) − (1 − c(j,i,t)) ·
β\_h(j,i,t) / τ\_r(j,i,t),

hardening at rate 1/τ\_h (Definition 10.5b.13b.2) against what is
classified extractive and relaxing at rate 1/τ\_r (Definition
10.5b.13b.6) toward what is not. Three classes of contact follow. Toward
an open counterpart, Danger\_true(i,t) ≈ 0 and a calibrated θ\_protect
gives c ≈ 0, so β\_h → 0 and open, bidirectional, non-extractive
exchange is admitted; such exchange is possible only between open
systems, since a closed or high-entropy counterpart has no open exchange
to offer, only extraction to seek. Against an extractive advance, c → 1
and β\_h → 1, admitting no extractive transfer. Neutral environmental
state changes --- ordinary fluctuations in the surrounding informational
state, carrying neither exchange nor extraction --- likewise have
Danger\_true ≈ 0, and a healthy boundary registers them without
hardening at all. B\_open (Definition 10.5b.0.6) is the limiting case of
this operator in a healthy container: β\_h(j,i,t) = 0 wherever c(j,i,t)
= 0 and β\_h(j,i,t) = 1 wherever c(j,i,t) = 1, i.e. maximal admissible
exchange, extractive exchange inadmissible. The healthiest boundary is
accordingly neither maximally open nor maximally closed, but maximally
selective.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13b.2 (Hardening Latency).}} Define

τ\_h(j,i,t) = τ\_min · {[}1 + κ\_lat · (1 − A\_acc(j,i,t)){]} /
g(Ψ\_total(j,t)), τ\_min, κ\_lat \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where g is Definition 10.5b.2.4's increasing gate, normalized so that
g(1) = 1, with g(Ψ\_total) = 0 for Ψ\_total \textless{} θ\_perceive.
Hardening latency thereby inherits the existing perception-and-action
gating rather than introducing a new mechanism: below θ\_perceive, τ\_h
= ∞ and the advance never registers; between θ\_perceive and θ\_act, g
is small and τ\_h is elevated; at high Ψ\_total and full observational
access, τ\_h → τ\_min, effectively instantaneous. Low observational
access to the counterpart's true state --- the condition a deceptive
predator produces --- lengthens τ\_h through the bracketed factor.
Low-entropy containers are characterized by τ\_h ≈ τ\_min: they harden
instantly against high-entropy advances while remaining open toward open
counterparts. Medium-entropy containers, though also open, are
characterized by elevated τ\_h, and that delay is the window in which
extraction and tipping momentum accrue.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13b.3 (Conversion Time).}} For an advance
by i on j beginning at t\_0, define the conversion time as the time the
advance would need to produce harm if j did not harden (β\_h ≡ 0):

τ\_conv(i,j,t\_0) = min\{τ\_X, τ\_M\}, τ\_X = inf\{s :
∫\_\{t\_0\}\^{}\{t\_0+s\} X\_\{i,j\}(u) du ≥ X\_floor\}, τ\_M = inf\{s :
∫\_\{t\_0\}\^{}\{t\_0+s\} μ · N\_B(j,u) du ≥ M\_floor\},

X\_floor, M\_floor \textgreater{} 0 fitted, where the second integral is
§10.5b.7's tipping-momentum integrand with route mass μ (μ\_p + μ\_m or
μ\_o + μ\_m, per the route). Under approximately constant flux and
drive, τ\_X ≈ X\_floor / X\_\{i,j\} and τ\_M ≈ M\_floor / (μ · N\_B(j)).
τ\_conv shortens with i's extractive intensity (through X\_\{i,j\}'s
dependence on Λ\_ij, 𝒱\_\{i→j\} and R\_j) and with the surrounding
grid's predation intensity, since Drive\_B(j,t) sums over every
concurrent pusher (§8.8a): the more high-entropy the grid, the faster
any advance converts.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13b.4 (The Kinetic Plane).}} Under an
advance that j classifies correctly (c ≈ 1) from an open start (β\_h = 0
at t\_0), the hardening dynamics of Definition 10.5b.13b.1 give β\_h(s)
= 1 − exp(−s/τ\_h), so that extraction actually realized over the
encounter is bounded:

∫\_\{t\_0\}\^{}∞ X\_\{i,j\}\^{}eff(u) du = X\_\{i,j\} · τ\_h

for constant flux, and correspondingly for momentum. Realized harm from
i stays below its floor if and only if τ\_h(j,i,t) \textless{}
τ\_conv(i,j,t) --- the condition is derived from the boundary dynamics,
not stipulated. Container j is below the kinetic plane of its grid at
time t when this holds for every active high-entropy container i in its
encounter set 𝒜\_j(t).

Substituting Definition 10.5b.13b.2, the condition for each i reads
g(Ψ\_total(j,t)) \textgreater{} τ\_min{[}1 + κ\_lat(1 −
A\_acc(j,i,t)){]} / τ\_conv(i,j,t). The kinetic plane is accordingly the
surface

Ψ\^{}*(j,t) = max\_\{i∈𝒜\_j(t)\} g\^{}\{−1\}( τ\_min{[}1 + κ\_lat(1 −
A\_acc(j,i,t)){]} / τ\_conv(i,j,t) ),

and j is below the plane exactly when Ψ\_total(j,t) \textgreater{}
Ψ\^{}*(j,t). The plane is grid-relative: Ψ\^{}* rises as the grid's
predation intensity shortens τ\_conv, and as a predator's deception
lowers j's access A\_acc(j,i,t). Where τ\_conv(i,j,t) \textless{}
τ\_min{[}1 + κ\_lat(1 − A\_acc(j,i,t)){]} for some i, the argument of
g\^{}\{−1\} exceeds g(1) = 1, no Ψ\_total satisfies the condition, and
the plane lies above every container: handling is not wholly within a
container's control, and at sufficient predation intensity no handling
strategy suffices (Corollary 10.5b.13b.9).

Being below the plane does not mean going unencountered. Define j's
encounter rate λ\_enc(j,t), set largely by the grid (Σ\_search\^{}eff,
§10.5b.11c.2) and by j's broadcast vulnerability (§8.6a.4), and its mean
realized extraction per encounter x̄(j,t) = (Σ\_\{i∈𝒜\_j\}
∫X\_\{i,j\}\^{}eff du) / N\_enc(j,t). The plane's observable signature
is x̄(j,t) ≤ X\_floor independent of λ\_enc(j,t): a container below the
plane may continue to attract high-entropy containers, but contact fails
to convert into harm. In a mixed grid a low-entropy container remains
below the plane largely by default; in a high-entropy grid, remaining
below it requires repelling advances on contact.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13b.5 (Analytic Mastery Does Not Lower
the Plane).}} Membership below the kinetic plane depends on
Ψ\_total(j,t), A\_acc(j,i,t), τ\_min and τ\_conv(i,j,t) alone
(Definition 10.5b.13b.4). None of these is a function of j's capacity to
describe predation dynamics: the partial derivative of Ψ\^{}*(j,t) and
of τ\_h(j,i,t) with respect to any articulated content of j's own model
of predation is zero, unless that content changes Ψ\_total or A\_acc
themselves. This formalizes the medium-entropy form of the sixth
miscalibration (§10.5b.2.20): a container that believes itself below the
plane because it can describe high-entropy dynamics fluently, while
Ψ\_total(j,t) remains below Ψ\^{}*(j,t).

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13b.6 (Reopening Latency and Boundary
Miscalibration).}} Selective hardening requires accurate classification
of what reaches the boundary. Define reopening latency,
history-dependent in keeping with Definition 2.3's generation of B\_t
from the container's history,

τ\_r(j,t) = τ\_r0 · (1 + κ\_sens · Z\_sens(j,t)), dZ\_sens/dt = η\_sens
· β̄\_h(j,t) · (1 − Z\_sens(j,t)) − λ\_sens · Z\_sens(j,t),

τ\_r0, κ\_sens, η\_sens, λ\_sens \textgreater{} 0 fitted, where
β̄\_h(j,t) is j's contact-weighted mean hardening state and Z\_sens ∈
{[}0,1{]} is a sensitization accumulator: sustained hardening sensitizes
the boundary, and sensitization decays at rate λ\_sens once hardening
subsides. Sensitization also lowers the protect threshold,

θ\_protect(j,t) = θ\_protect\^{}ref − κ\_θ · Z\_sens(j,t), κ\_θ ≥ 0
fitted.

A healthy container exhibits both low τ\_h and low τ\_r: it hardens
instantly against extraction and stands down once the threat has passed.

Boundary miscalibration takes two structurally distinct forms. In
misclassification, the boundary hardens against an open counterpart or a
neutral change that never was a threat. With perceived signal ε from a
contact whose Danger\_true ≈ 0, its classification is c = 1/\{1 +
exp{[}−κ\_c(ε − θ\_protect){]}\}, so the false-positive rate

FP(j,t) = 𝔼{[}c(j,k,t) Danger\_true(k,t) ≈ 0{]}

rises monotonically as θ\_protect is displaced downward ---
§10.5b.2.20's threshold-shift formalization, applied at the boundary. In
persistence, the hardening was correct when it occurred, but λ\_sens ≈
0, so that Z\_sens does not decay once the threat has resolved: τ\_r
stays elevated, β\_h relaxes slowly or not at all, and because Z\_sens
also lowers θ\_protect, persistence feeds misclassification, the
retained protective configuration extending to neutral changes that
reach the boundary afterward. Persistence parallels Proposition
10.5b.2.20a: the original protective response is functionally sound, and
the miscalibration is its residue outlasting its purpose. A biological
instance is the cell danger response, which begins as appropriate
protection against a genuine threat and becomes harmful when it fails to
stand down after the threat resolves.

Neither form is costless. Hardening is work: define the defense cost

W\_def(j,t) = κ\_def · Σ\_k β\_h(j,k,t) · n\_k(t), κ\_def \textgreater{}
0 fitted,

where n\_k(t) is the intensity of contact from k, entering §7.4's
balance through M\_add(t) inside total work W(t) (§7.3), so that defense
is clipped jointly with every other draw: dR/dt = P\_prod − W(t) +
E\_exch\^{}adm. The portion of W\_def spent on contacts with
Danger\_true ≈ 0 purchases no protection and accumulates as resource
debt (§7.9) from defense alone, with no external source of harm; it can
arise within a single subsystem's boundary while the container as a
whole remains open. Where miscalibration instead generalizes across the
container's outer boundary, so that j's mean hardening toward open
counterparts β̄\_h\^{}open(j,t) → 1, admissible open exchange falls
toward zero and the container drifts toward closure --- an instance of
§10.5b.17's sticky binary trust sorting, applied at the boundary. A
container with low τ\_h and unbounded τ\_r across its outer boundary has
not remained healthy under pressure. It has become closed, and where it
supplies its own stability through extraction, high-entropy (Proposition
10.5b.13a.3, option (i)).

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13b.7 (Defensive Modes).}} The means by
which a container remains below the kinetic plane are classified by
their timing relative to contact. Each acts on a specific term of
existing machinery, and each reduces to the prior formulation when its
own coefficient is zero.

Before contact: containment shielding and cloaking (Definition
10.5b.13b.8).

On contact, four modes. \emph{Refusal} declines to form or sustain the
coupling: it adds −κ\_ref · β\_h(j,i,t) · Λ\_ij(t) to Λ\_ij's own
dynamics (§8.5) and −κ\_ref · β\_h(j,i,t) inside ρ(j,t)'s logistic
argument (Definition 8.6a.1), so that both coupling strength and
coupling persistence fall, and X\_\{i,j\} with them. \emph{Currency
withholding} remains in contact while withholding the specific resource
the advance seeks: R\_j in §8.6a's extraction flux is replaced by
R\_j\^{}exp(t) = (1 − s\_res(j,i,t)) · R\_j(t), s\_res ∈ {[}0,1{]} the
withheld fraction, so that realized extraction falls to zero at s\_res =
1 despite contact. \emph{Repulsion signaling} raises i's estimated cost
of pursuing j: Definition 10.5b.11c.1a's selection is extended to

Select(i,t) = argmax\_k {[} w\_prox · Prox(i,k,t) + w\_type ·
𝒱\_\{i→k\}(t) − w\_cost · C\_pursuit(i,k,t) {]}, w\_prox + w\_type +
w\_cost = 1,

where C\_pursuit(i,k,t) ≥ 0 is i's estimate of the cost of pursuing k,
raised by k's signaling; w\_cost = 0 recovers the original selection.
\emph{Credible consequence} recruits an external anchor or governance
structure (§10.5b.13a, Definition 6.1.1) to raise the expected cost of
continued extraction above its yield. Define i's net expected yield from
j

Yld\_\{i,j\}(t) = X\_\{i,j\}(t) − p\_exp(i,j,t) · L\_exp(i,t),

where p\_exp is the probability that continued extraction triggers
exposure or sanction through the recruited structure and L\_exp(i,t) is
i's loss under exposure; extraction persists only while the yield
remains positive, X\_\{i,j\} being multiplied by 1/\{1 + exp{[}−κ\_Y ·
Yld\_\{i,j\}(t){]}\}, κ\_Y \textgreater{} 0 fitted. For a high-entropy
container whose stability depends on concealment, L\_exp(i,t) is
characteristically large relative to any single extraction yield, since
exposure collapses the M\_decep(t) on which its official layer rests
(§10.5b.18.4); this is why credible consequence is characteristically
most effective against such containers. Unlike the other three modes, it
draws on force from outside the encounter rather than at the boundary
itself, and it can secure the conditions for flight (Proposition
